Involutive quantales and quantale-enriched involutive topological spaces Thanks: The first named author acknowledges support from the Basque Government (grant IT1483-22).
Javier Gutiérrez García, Ulrich Höhle

Abstract
. In this paper, we provide a comprehensive analysis of involutive quantales, with a particular focus on quantic frames. We extend the axiomatic foundations of quantale-enriched topological spaces to include closure under the anti-homomorphic involution, facilitating a balanced topologization of the spectrum of unital C ∗ C^{*} -algebras that encompasses both closed right and left ideals through the concept of quantic frames. Specifically, certain subspaces of pure states are identified as strongly Hausdorff separated, involutive quantale-enriched topological spaces.



†
†
footnotetext:
MSC2020: Primary 18F75 Secondary 06F07, 18D20.

Introduction

Quantales are semigroups in the symmetric, monoidal, closed category of complete lattices and join-preserving maps. Involutive quantales are quantales with an involution that is both anti-homomorphic and join-preserving. It is well known that not all quantales admit such involutions.
A typical counterexample is a non-commutative quantale defined on a complete chain. However, every quantale possessing an idempotent element distinct from the universal lower bound (e.g. a balanced quantale) can be embedded into an involutive quantale (see Remark in Sec.
2
). This observation highlights the significance of unitalization, through which any quantale can be realized as a subquantale of some involutive quantale.
Therefore, the assumption of an anti-homomorphic and join-preserving involution should not be viewed as a restrictive condition. Rather, it reflects that the quantale in question has an appropriate structural “size”.

Further, every strongly spatial quantale can be characterized
by a special type of quantale-enriched topological space — a so-called quantized topological space
(cf. Proposition
4.2
).
Since the spectra of non-commutative and unital
C^{*}
-algebras are expressed by involutive quantales (cf.
[
16
, Def. 2.2]
and
[
3
, p. 157]
), this situation motivates the extension of the axiomatic foundations of quantale-enriched topological spaces.

This extension ensures that quantale-enriched topologies are also closed under the anti-homomorphic and join-preserving involution of the underlying unital and involutive quantale.
This approach leads to a change in the concept of subbase and in all those notions depending on subbases — e.g. weak regularity (cf.
[
1
, Def. 6.5]
).
On the other hand, we gain a natural topologization of the spectrum of a unital
C^{*}
-algebra
A
, which includes not only all closed right ideals of
A

(as in the approach considered by Mulvey and Pelletier (cf.
[
17
, Sec. 7]
)), but also all closed left ideals of
A
.
This balanced treatment of closed left and closed right ideals in the topologization process is
achieved through the application of the important concept of quantic frames (cf.
[
3
, Def. 2.5.13]
) —
a concept which reduces to traditional frames provided the quantale multiplication is commutative. Therefore, from the perspective of applications, this axiomatic extension of quantale-enriched topologies seems to be reasonable and leads consequently to the concept of involutive quantale-enriched topological spaces.

The paper is organized as follows. We begin with some preliminaries on quantales. In Section
2
, we present a fundamental theorem on the construction of involutive quantales, originating from
[
3
, Thm. 2.3.33]
. As an application, we introduce the concept of a
tensorially involutive
quantale associated with an involutive and semi-unital quantale. Furthermore, in the case of quantale epimorphisms with an involutive quantale as its domain, we characterize the existence of an anti-homomorphic and join-preserving involution on its codomain. This result transforms the given epimorphism into an involutive quantale homomorphism. As an application we present a formulation of the spectrum of a unital and non-commutative
C^{*}
-algebra, which is equivalent to the traditional spectrum of
C^{*}
-algebras in the commutative case. It is interesting to see that in the framework of the category of balanced and bisymmetric quantales with strong quantale homomorphisms this construction has a categorical base. In this sense, the spectrum of a unital
C^{*}
-algebra is a
quantic frame
. This means, among other things, that its spectrum is uniquely determined by its left-sided and right-sided elements in the form of a pushout square.

Subsequently, we recall the axioms of a quantale-enriched topological space including the lower separation axioms, and introduce the concept of involutive quantale-enriched topological spaces. Since the quantization of
\boldsymbol{2}
is an involutive quantale, involutive quantized topological spaces form an important subclass of this new type of structure. It is remarkable to see that the strong Hausdorff separation axiom has a convergent-theoretical characterization in the case of quantized topologies. Moreover, every involutive quantale has a topological representation by an involutive quantized topological space (cf. Proposition
4.3
). An application of this construction to quantic frames, and especially to the spectrum of non-commutative
C^{*}
-algebras, is given. In the case of the
C^{*}
-algebra
B(\mathcal{H})
of all bounded, linear operators on a Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
, a strongly Hausdorff separated, involutive quantized topological space is presented, whose underlying set depends on the Hilbert space dimension of
\mathcal{H}
.

1.
Preliminaries on quantales

in
\mathsf{Set}
which is join-preserving in each variable separately. In the following,

\ast
will be called the
quantale multiplication
of
\mathfrak{Q}
, and so the
left-
and
right-implication
corresponding to
\ast
are given by:

\alpha\swarrow\beta=\mathop{\textstyle\bigvee}\{\,\gamma\in\mathfrak{Q}\mid\gamma\ast\beta\leq\alpha\,\}\quad\text{and}\quad\alpha\searrow\beta=\mathop{\textstyle\bigvee}\{\,\gamma\in\mathfrak{Q}\mid\alpha\ast\gamma\leq\beta\,\},\qquad\alpha,\beta\in\mathfrak{Q}.

An element
\delta
of a quantale is
dualizing
if
\delta
satisfies the following property for all
\alpha\in\mathfrak{Q}
:

\delta\swarrow(\alpha\searrow\delta)=\alpha=(\delta\swarrow\alpha)\searrow\delta.

A quantale
(\mathfrak{Q},m)
is
balanced
if its universal upper bound is idempotent — i.e.
\top\ast\top=\top
.

(\mathfrak{Q},m)
and
(\mathfrak{R},n)
be quantales.

is called a
quantale homomorphism
if the following diagram is commutative:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{Q}\otimes\mathfrak{Q}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{R}\otimes\mathfrak{R}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{Q}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{R}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h\otimes h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces n}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}

i.e. if
h(\alpha\ast\beta)=h(\alpha)\ast h(\beta)
for each
\alpha,\beta\in\mathfrak{Q}
.
Consequently quantales and quantale homomorphisms form a category denoted by
\mathsf{Quant}
. A quantale homomorphism
h
is
strong
if
h
preserves the respective universal upper bounds — i.e.
h(\top)=\top
.

A quantale
(\mathfrak{R},n)
is a
subquantale
of
(\mathfrak{Q},m)
if
\mathfrak{R}
is a subset of
\mathfrak{Q}
and the inclusion map
\mathfrak{R}\xhookrightarrow{\,\,\,}\mathfrak{Q}
is a monomorphism in
\mathsf{Quant}
.

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\mathfrak{R}
is called a
quantale anti-homomorphism
if the following diagram is commutative:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{Q}\otimes\mathfrak{Q}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{R}\otimes\mathfrak{R}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{R}\otimes\mathfrak{R}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{Q}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{R}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h\otimes h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{\mathfrak{R}\mathfrak{R}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces n}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}

where
c_{\mathfrak{R}\mathfrak{R}}
is the
\mathfrak{R}\mathfrak{R}
-component of the symmetry in
\mathsf{Sup}
— i.e. if
h(\alpha\ast\beta)=h(\beta)\ast h(\alpha)
for each
\alpha,\beta\in\mathfrak{Q}
.

A quantale
(\mathfrak{Q},m)
is called
involutive
if there exists a quantale anti-homomorphism
\mathfrak{Q}\mathrel{\hbox to13.35pt{\vbox to14.89pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 3.67326pt\lower-5.01602pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.7pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}{}{}{{{}{}}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{}{{{}{}}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 7.33 0}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{5.65002pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 7.82 0)} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray=none,stroke-dashoffset=0.0pt} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap=round} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin=round} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -3.38 4.07 C -2.76 1.63 -1.38 0.47 0 0 C -1.38 -0.47 -2.76 -1.63 -3.38 -4.07}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{

\lxSVG@closescope }}}\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{}{{

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-0.34026pt}{1.68301pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 -0.47 2.33)} \pgfsys@hbox{58}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\mathfrak{Q}
satisfying
\ell\circ\ell=1_{\mathfrak{Q}}
. Then we write
(\mathfrak{Q},m,\ell)
and call
\ell
also an
involution
on
\mathfrak{Q}
. A join-preserving map
\mathfrak{Q}\mathrel{\hbox to14.68pt{\vbox to14.89pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 4.3405pt\lower-5.01602pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.7pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{{}}{}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{3.0pt}{-1.68301pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 4.15 -2.33)} \pgfsys@hbox{58}\lxSVG@closescope }}}

}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-1.00749pt}{1.68301pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 -1.39 2.33)} \pgfsys@hbox{58}\lxSVG@closescope }}}

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\mathfrak{R}
between involutive quantales is involutive if the following diagram is commutative:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{Q}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{R}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{Q}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{R}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ell_{\mathfrak{Q}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ell_{\mathfrak{R}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}

i.e. if
h(\alpha^{\prime})=h(\alpha)^{\prime}
for each
\alpha\in\mathfrak{Q}
. It is not difficult to show that a join-preserving map
\mathfrak{Q}\mathrel{\hbox to14.68pt{\vbox to14.89pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 4.3405pt\lower-5.01602pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.7pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}{}{}{{{}{}}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{}{{{}{}}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 7.33 0}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{5.65002pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 7.82 0)} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray=none,stroke-dashoffset=0.0pt} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap=round} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin=round} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -3.38 4.07 C -2.76 1.63 -1.38 0.47 0 0 C -1.38 -0.47 -2.76 -1.63 -3.38 -4.07}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{}{{

{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{3.0pt}{-1.68301pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 4.15 -2.33)} \pgfsys@hbox{58}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{}{{

{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-1.00749pt}{1.68301pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 -1.39 2.33)} \pgfsys@hbox{58}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\mathfrak{R}
is involutive if and only if the right adjoint map
h^{\vdash}
of
h
is involutive (cf.
[
3
, Lem. 2.2.14]
).

An element
\alpha
of an involutive quantale is called
hermitian
if
\alpha=\alpha^{\prime}
holds. A subquantale
\mathfrak{S}
of an involutive quantale
\mathfrak{Q}
is called
involutive
if
\alpha^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{S}
for all
\alpha\in\mathfrak{S}
.

Let
(\mathfrak{Q},m)
be a quantale.
The
opposite quantale
of
(\mathfrak{Q},m)
is the quantale
(\mathfrak{Q},m^{op})
where
m^{op}
is determined by the following commutative diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{Q}\otimes\mathfrak{Q}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{Q}\otimes\mathfrak{Q}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{Q}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m^{{o\!p}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{\mathfrak{Q}\mathfrak{Q}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m}

In the following we will call
m^{op}
the
opposite binary operation
of
m
and denote
the corresponding quantale multiplication
by
\ast^{op}
— i.e.
\alpha\mathop{\ast^{op}}\beta=\beta\ast\alpha
for each
\alpha,\beta\in\mathfrak{Q}
.
Obviously,
m^{op}
coincides with
m
if and only if
(\mathfrak{Q},m)
is commutative.

A
unital
quantale
(\mathfrak{Q},m)
is a monoid in
\mathsf{Sup}
. We identify the unit
\mathds{1}\mathrel{\hbox to13.79pt{\vbox to13.05pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 3.89668pt\lower-5.01602pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.7pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}{}{}{{{}{}}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{}{{{}{}}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 7.33 0}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{5.65002pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 7.82 0)} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray=none,stroke-dashoffset=0.0pt} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap=round} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin=round} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -3.38 4.07 C -2.76 1.63 -1.38 0.47 0 0 C -1.38 -0.47 -2.76 -1.63 -3.38 -4.07}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{3.0pt}{-1.68301pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 4.15 -2.33)} \pgfsys@hbox{58}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-0.56367pt}{1.68301pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 -0.78 2.33)} \pgfsys@hbox{58}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\mathfrak{Q}
with the element
e(1)\in\mathfrak{Q}
, which we will also denote by
e
. Therefore, we write
(\mathfrak{Q},m,e)
for a unital quantale. The tensor product of unital quantales is again unital.

Let
\mathfrak{Q}=(\mathfrak{Q},m)
be a quantale, and let
\ast
denote the corresponding quantale multiplication. Then
\mathfrak{Q}
is said to have the following properties:

–

left-sided
if every element
\alpha\in\mathfrak{Q}
is left-sided — i.e.
\top\ast\alpha\leq\alpha
.

–

right-sided
if every element
\alpha\in\mathfrak{Q}
is right-sided — i.e.
\alpha\ast\top\leq\alpha
.

–

two-sided
if
\mathfrak{Q}
is left-sided
and
right-sided.

–

pre-idempotent
if every element
\alpha\in\mathfrak{Q}
is pre-idempotent — i.e.
\alpha\leq\alpha\ast\alpha
.

–

idempotent
if every element
\alpha\in\mathfrak{Q}
is idempotent — i.e.
\alpha=\alpha\ast\alpha
.

–

semi-unital
if
\alpha\leq\top\ast\alpha
and
\alpha\leq\alpha\ast\top
for all
\alpha\in\mathfrak{Q}
.

–

semi-integral
if
\alpha\ast\top\ast\beta\leq\alpha\ast\beta
for all
\alpha,\beta\in\mathfrak{Q}
.

–

bisymmetric
if
\alpha\ast\beta_{1}\ast\beta_{2}\ast\gamma=\alpha\ast\beta_{2}\ast\beta_{1}\ast\gamma
for all
\alpha,\beta_{1},\beta_{2},\gamma\in\mathfrak{Q}
.

Every unital (resp. pre-idempotent) quantale is semi-unital, every semi-unital quantale is balanced, and every left-sided (resp. right-sided) quantale is semi-integral. The subquantale of
\mathfrak{Q}
of all left-sided (resp. right-sided, two-sided) elements of
\mathfrak{Q}
is denoted by
\mathds{L}(\mathfrak{Q})
(resp.
\mathds{R}(\mathfrak{Q})
,
\mathds{I}(\mathfrak{Q})
).
A quantale
\mathfrak{Q}
is a
factor
if
\mathds{I}(\mathfrak{Q})
contains only the universal bounds of
\mathfrak{Q}
— i.e.
\mathds{I}(\mathfrak{Q})=\{\,\bot,\top\,\}
.

Lemma 1.1
.

Let
\mathfrak{Q}
be a semi-unital and semi-integral quantale. If
\mathfrak{Q}
is a factor, then the following property holds for all
\alpha,\beta\in\mathfrak{Q}
:

\alpha\ast\gamma\ast\beta=\alpha\ast\beta,\qquad\gamma\in\mathfrak{Q}\setminus\{\bot\}.

Proof.

Let us consider
\gamma\in\mathfrak{Q}\setminus\{\bot\}
. Then
\top\ast\gamma\ast\top\in\mathds{I}(\mathfrak{Q})
, and since
\mathfrak{Q}
is a factor and semi-unital, we obtain
\top\ast\gamma\ast\top=\top
. Finally, since
\mathfrak{Q}
is semi-integral and semi-unital, we conclude that

\alpha\ast\gamma\ast\beta\leq\alpha\ast\top\ast\beta\leq\alpha\ast\beta\leq\alpha\ast\top\ast\beta=\alpha\ast\top\ast\gamma\ast\top\ast\beta\leq\alpha\ast\gamma\ast\beta.\qed

Further,
\mathfrak{Q}

does not have zero divisors if,
for
\alpha\neq\bot
and
\beta\neq\bot
, the relation
\alpha\ast\beta\neq\bot
follows.

Lemma 1.2
.

Let
\mathfrak{Q}
be a semi-unital quantale.

(a)

If
\mathds{I}(\mathfrak{Q})
does not have zero divisors, then
\mathds{L}(\mathfrak{Q})

(
resp.
\mathds{R}(\mathfrak{Q})
)
also does not have zero divisors.

(b)

If
\mathds{L}(\mathfrak{Q})
does not have zero divisors, then for
\alpha\in\mathfrak{Q}\setminus\{\bot\}
and for
\beta\in\mathds{L}(\mathfrak{Q})\setminus\{\bot\}
the relation
\alpha\ast\beta\neq\bot
holds.

(c)

If
\mathds{R}(\mathfrak{Q})
does not have zero divisors, then for
\alpha\in\mathds{R}(\mathfrak{Q)}\setminus\{\bot\}
and for
\beta\in\mathfrak{Q}\setminus\{\bot\}
the relation
\beta\ast\alpha\neq\bot
holds.

Proof.

Let
\alpha,\beta\in\mathds{L}(\mathfrak{Q})
such that
\alpha\ast\beta=\bot
. Then
\alpha\ast\beta\ast\top=\bot
,
and the left-sidedness of
\beta
implies
(\alpha\ast\top)\ast(\beta\ast\top)=\bot
.
Since
\alpha\ast\top
and
\beta\ast\top
are two-sided and
\mathds{I}(\mathfrak{Q})
does not
have zero divisors, we obtain
\alpha\ast\top=\bot
or
\beta\ast\top=\bot
,
and so
\alpha=\bot
or
\beta=\bot
follows — i.e.
\mathds{L}(\mathfrak{Q})
does not have zero divisors.
The case
\mathds{R}(\mathfrak{Q})
can be treated analogously.
The verification of (b) and (c) is left to the reader.
∎

A prominent example of a left-sided quantale which does not have zero divisors, is given in Example
1.8
infra.

An element
p
of a quantale
\mathfrak{Q}
is called
prime
if
p\neq\top
and for all
\alpha,\beta\in\mathfrak{Q}
the following implication holds (cf.
[
3
, p. 100]
):

\text{If }\alpha\ast\beta\leq p,\text{ then }\alpha\ast\top\leq p\text{ or }\top\ast\beta\leq p.

A prime element of a quantale
\mathfrak{Q}
is
strongly prime
if for all
\alpha,\beta\in\mathfrak{Q}
the stronger implication holds (cf.
[
3
, p. 150]
):

\text{If }\alpha\ast\beta\leq p,\text{ then }\alpha\vee(\alpha\ast\top)\leq p\text{ or }\beta\vee(\top\ast\beta)\leq p.

The set
\sigma(\mathfrak{Q})
(resp.
\sigma_{s}(\mathfrak{Q})
) of all prime (resp. strongly prime)
elements is called the
spectrum
(resp.
strong spectrum
) of
\mathfrak{Q}
.
The
hermitian spectrum
of an involutive quantale
(\mathfrak{Q},m,\ell)
is the set
\sigma_{h}(\mathfrak{Q})
of all hermitian and strongly prime elements.

A quantale
\mathfrak{Q}
is called
strongly spatial
(resp.
spatial
), if every element of
\mathfrak{Q}
is a meet of strongly prime (resp. prime) elements of
\mathfrak{Q}
.

Remarks 1.3
.

(1) If
\mathfrak{Q}
is semi-unital, then every prime element is strongly prime — i.e.
\sigma_{s}(\mathfrak{Q})=\sigma(\mathfrak{Q})
. Hence any spatial and semi-unital quantale is strongly spatial.

(2) Let
\widehat{\mathfrak{Q}}
be the semi-unitalization of
\mathfrak{Q}
(cf.
[
3
, pp. 98, 150]
) — i.e.
\mathfrak{Q}
is extended by an idempotent element
\widehat{\top}
, which serves as the universal upper bound in
\widehat{\mathfrak{Q}}=\mathfrak{Q}\cup\{\widehat{\top}\}
and interacts with
\mathfrak{Q}
as follows:

\alpha\ast\widehat{\top}:=\alpha\vee(\alpha\ast\top)\quad\text{ and }\quad\widehat{\top}\ast\alpha:=\alpha\vee(\top\ast\alpha),\qquad\alpha\in\mathfrak{Q}.

Obviously
\widehat{\mathfrak{Q}}
is semi-unital, and the universal upper bound
\top
in
\mathfrak{Q}
is prime and two-sided in
\widehat{\mathfrak{Q}}
.
Moreover,
\sigma\bigl(\widehat{\mathfrak{Q}}\bigr)=\sigma_{s}\bigl(\widehat{\mathfrak{Q}}\bigr)=\sigma_{s}(\mathfrak{Q})\cup\{\top\}
, hence
\mathfrak{Q}
is strongly spatial if and only if
\widehat{\mathfrak{Q}}
is spatial.

(3) Let
L
be a complete lattice and let
[L,L]
be the quantale of all join-preserving self-maps
L\mathrel{\hbox to11.67pt{\vbox to10.03pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 2.83295pt\lower-5.01602pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.7pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}{}{}{{{}{}}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{}{{{}{}}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 7.33 0}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{5.65002pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 7.82 0)} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray=none,stroke-dashoffset=0.0pt} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap=round} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin=round} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -3.38 4.07 C -2.76 1.63 -1.38 0.47 0 0 C -1.38 -0.47 -2.76 -1.63 -3.38 -4.07}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{3.0pt}{-1.68301pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 4.15 -2.33)} \pgfsys@hbox{58}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{0.50006pt}{1.68301pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 0.69 2.33)} \pgfsys@hbox{58}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}L
ordered pointwise and provided with the composition as quantale multiplication. Then
[L,L]
is semi-unital and a factor. If
L
contains at least
three
elements, then
[L,L]
is
not semi-integral
and the spectrum of
\mathfrak{Q}
is
empty
.

Concerning the
existence
of prime elements, the
semi-integrality
of quantales plays a significant role (see e.g., Remarks
1.3
(3)). We quote the following fact (cf.
[
3
, Thm. 2.3.21]
.

Fact 1.4
.

Every maximal left-sided
(
resp. right-sided
)
element of a semi-integral quantale is prime.

Recall that every spatial quantale is semi-integral and bisymmetric (cf.
[
8
, Lem. 1.3 (i)]
).
The next proposition summarizes some properties of strongly spatial quantales.

Proposition 1.5
.

Let
\mathfrak{Q}
be strongly spatial. Then
:

(i)

\mathfrak{Q}
is semi-unital.

(ii)

\mathfrak{Q}
is pre-idempotent.

(iii)

If
\mathfrak{Q}
is either left-sided or right-sided, then
\mathfrak{Q}
is idempotent.

Proof.

To verify (i), we fix
\alpha\in\mathfrak{Q}
and observe that if
p
is strongly prime in
\mathfrak{Q}
such that
\alpha\ast\top\leq p
then
\alpha\leq p
. Consequently,
\alpha\leq\mathop{\textstyle\bigwedge}\{\,p\in\sigma_{s}(\mathfrak{Q})\mid\alpha\ast\top\leq p\,\}=\alpha\ast\top
.
Analogously, we can verify
\alpha\leq\top\ast\alpha
. The remaining properties follow from (i) and
[
8
, Lem. 1.3 (ii) and (iii)]
.
∎

With regard to Proposition
1.5
(i) the following equivalence holds.

Corollary 1.6
.

A quantale
\mathfrak{Q}
is strongly spatial if and only if it is spatial and semi-unital.

Comment 1.7
.

There exist spatial quantales, which are
not
semi-unital. The simplest example is the trivial quantale on the
3
-chain
C_{3}=\{\,\bot,a,\top\,\}
with
\bot<a<\top
— i.e. the quantale multiplication is determined by
\top\ast\top=\bot
. Since
\bot
and
a
are prime elements, it follows that it is a spatial quantale. However,
\bot
is not strongly prime, hence the quantale fails to be strongly spatial.

Before we proceed we present an example illustrating the previous results.

Example 1.8
.

Let
\mathcal{H}
be an infinite dimensional Hilbert space and
B(\mathcal{H})
be the unital
C^{*}
-algebra of all bounded linear operators on
\mathcal{H}
. Further, let
\mathds{L}(B(\mathcal{H}))
be the complete lattice of all closed left ideals of
B(\mathcal{H})
ordered by set-inclusion and provided with the usual ideal multiplication:

I_{1}\ast I_{2}=\text{top.\ closure}\bigl(\bigl\{\,\mathop{\textstyle\sum}\limits_{i=1}^{n}f_{i}\cdot g_{i}\mid n\in\mathds{N},\,f_{i}\in I_{1},\,g_{i}\in I_{2}\,\bigr\}\bigr),\qquad I_{1},I_{2}\in\mathds{L}(B(\mathcal{H})).

Then
\mathds{L}(B(\mathcal{H}))=(\mathds{L}(B(\mathcal{H})),\ast)
is a left-sided quantale. With regard to Fact
1.4
, every
maximal
left ideal is
prime
. Since every closed left ideal of
B(\mathcal{H})
is an intersection of maximal left ideals (cf.
[
13
, Thm. 10.2.10 (iii)]
), the quantale
\mathds{L}(B(\mathcal{H}))
is spatial. Now, using the fact that
B(\mathcal{H})
is unital, we conclude that
\mathds{L}(B(\mathcal{H}))
is
semi-unital
. Hence
\mathds{L}(B(\mathcal{H}))
is strongly spatial, and consequently, Proposition
1.5
(iii) implies that the ideal multiplication is idempotent.

We now show that the ideal multiplication does not have zero divisors. With regard to Lemma
1.2
(a), it is sufficient to verify that the subquantale
\mathds{I}(B(\mathcal{H}))
of all closed two-sided ideals of
B(\mathcal{H})
does not have zero divisors. Since every non-zero closed two-sided ideal contains the closed two-sided ideal of all compact operators acting on
\mathcal{H}
(cf.
[
13
, p. 747]
), the property of not having zero divisors follows immediately from the idempotency of the ideal multiplication. Hence, the zero ideal is a (strongly) prime element of
\mathds{L}(B(\mathcal{H}))
. Analogously, it can be shown that the quantale
\mathds{R}(B(\mathcal{H}))
of all closed right ideals does not have zero divisors, and so the zero ideal is also a (strongly) prime element of
\mathds{R}(B(\mathcal{H}))
.

Further, it is well known that every unit vector
x\in\mathcal{H}
induces a pure state of
B(\mathcal{H})
by (cf.
[
12
, Exercise 4.6.68]
):

B(\mathcal{H})\mathrel{\hbox to19.5pt{\vbox to14.05pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 6.7477pt\lower-5.01602pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.7pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{{}}{}

where

is the inner product of
\mathcal{H}
. Since the one-to-one relationship between pure states
\varrho
and maximal left ideals
I
of
B(\mathcal{H})
is given by means of the construction of left kernels (cf.
[
13
, Thm. 10.2.10 (i) and (ii)]
):

I_{\varrho}=\{\,f\in B(\mathcal{H})\mid\varrho(f^{*}\cdot f)=0\,\},

every vector state
\omega_{x}
determines a maximal left ideal
I_{x}
by

I_{x}=\{\,f\in B(\mathcal{H})\mid\langle(f^{*}\cdot f)(x),x\rangle=0\,\}=\{\,f\in B(\mathcal{H})\mid f(x)=\vec{0}\,\}.

We end this section by recalling that the tensor product of quantales
(\mathfrak{Q},m)
and
(\mathfrak{R},n)
is given by the tensor product

of the underlying lattices, provided with an appropriate binary and associative operation

The quantale multiplication
\star
corresponding to
m\otimes n
is uniquely determined on elementary tensors of
\mathfrak{Q}\otimes\mathfrak{R}
as follows (cf.
[
3
, p. 92]
):

(\alpha_{1}\otimes\beta_{1})\star(\alpha_{2}\otimes\beta_{2})=(\alpha_{1}\ast\alpha_{2})\otimes(\beta_{1}\ast\beta_{2}),\qquad\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}\in\mathfrak{Q},\,\beta_{1},\beta_{2}\in\mathfrak{R}.

The
canonical
embeddings
\mathfrak{Q}\mathrel{\hbox to19.35pt{\vbox to16.64pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 6.6759pt\lower-5.01602pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.7pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{{}}{}

\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\mathfrak{Q}\otimes\mathfrak{R}
, defined by

j_{\mathfrak{Q}}(\alpha)=\alpha\otimes\top\quad\text{and}\quad j_{\mathfrak{R}}(\beta)=\top\otimes\beta,\qquad\alpha\in\mathfrak{Q},\,\beta\in\mathfrak{R},

are strong quantale homomorphisms if and only if both quantales
(\mathfrak{Q},m)
and
(\mathfrak{R},n)
are balanced.

The following permanence properties of the tensor product of quantales hold. If both quantales are pre-idempotent (resp. semi-unital, semi-integral, bisymmetric, and being a factor),
then
their tensor product again is pre-idempotent (resp. semi-unital, semi-integral, bisymmetric and a factor).
In particular, the tensor product of a left-sided quantale with a right-sided quantale is always semi-integral.

Finally, we recall a characterization of prime elements in the tensor product of quantales (cf.
[
3
, Lem. 2.3.22 and Thm. 2.3.25]
).

Theorem 1.9
.

Let
(\mathfrak{Q},m)
and
(\mathfrak{R},n)
be semi-unital quantales such that
(\mathfrak{Q},m)
is left-sided and
(\mathfrak{R},n)
is right-sided. Then an element
p\in\mathfrak{Q}\otimes\mathfrak{R}
is a prime element of
(\mathfrak{Q}\otimes\mathfrak{R},m\otimes n)
if and only if there exist prime elements
q
in
(\mathfrak{Q},m)
and
r
in
(\mathfrak{R},n)
such that
p=(q\otimes\top)\vee(\top\otimes r)
. Moreover,
q
and
r
are uniquely determined by
p
.

2.
On the existence of involutive quantales

Since every commutative quantale is involutive, the existence of non-commutative but involutive quantales represents an interesting feature in the theory of quantales.

It is well known that with every quantale homomorphism
(\mathfrak{Q},m)\mathrel{\hbox to14.68pt{\vbox to14.89pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 4.3405pt\lower-5.01602pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.7pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{{}}{}

is the right adjoint map of
h
. A nucleus on an involutive quantale
(\mathfrak{Q},m,\ell)
is involutive if and only if
c(\alpha)^{\prime}\leq c(\alpha^{\prime})
for all
\alpha\in\mathfrak{Q}
.

Proposition 2.1
.

Let
(\mathfrak{Q},m,\ell)
be an involutive quantale, and let
(\mathfrak{Q},m)\mathrel{\hbox to14.68pt{\vbox to13.05pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 4.33778pt\lower-5.01602pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.7pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}{}{}{{{}{}}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{}{{{}{}}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 7.33 0}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{5.65002pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 7.82 0)} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray=none,stroke-dashoffset=0.0pt} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap=round} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin=round} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -3.38 4.07 C -2.76 1.63 -1.38 0.47 0 0 C -1.38 -0.47 -2.76 -1.63 -3.38 -4.07}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{3.0pt}{-1.68301pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 4.15 -2.33)} \pgfsys@hbox{58}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-1.00478pt}{1.68301pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 -1.39 2.33)} \pgfsys@hbox{58}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}(\mathfrak{R},n)
be an epimorphism in the sense of
\mathsf{Quant}

(
in particular,
\pi
is surjective as map
)
.

Then the nucleus associated with
\pi
is involutive if and only if there exists an involution on
(\mathfrak{R},n)
such that
\pi
is an involutive quantale homomorphism.

Proof.

Sufficiency is evident. To verify necessity,
we proceed as follows. Let

c
be the nucleus associated with
\pi
. Define the map
\mathfrak{R}\mathrel{\hbox to18.69pt{\vbox to15.89pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 6.34691pt\lower-5.01602pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.7pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}{}{}{{{}{}}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{}{{{}{}}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 7.33 0}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{5.65002pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 7.82 0)} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray=none,stroke-dashoffset=0.0pt} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap=round} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin=round} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -3.38 4.07 C -2.76 1.63 -1.38 0.47 0 0 C -1.38 -0.47 -2.76 -1.63 -3.38 -4.07}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{3.0pt}{-1.68301pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 4.15 -2.33)} \pgfsys@hbox{58}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-3.0139pt}{2.68301pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 -4.17 3.71)} \pgfsys@hbox{58}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\mathfrak{R}
by

\ell_{\mathfrak{R}}:=\pi\circ\ell\circ\pi^{\vdash}.

This map is obviously isotone. Since
c
is involutive, the following relation holds:

(2.1)

\ell_{\mathfrak{R}}\circ\pi=\pi\circ\ell\circ c=\pi\circ c\circ\ell=\pi\circ\ell.

Since
\ell
is an involution and
\pi
is surjective, we have
\ell_{\mathfrak{R}}\circ\ell_{\mathfrak{R}}=\pi\circ\ell\circ\ell\circ\pi^{\vdash}=\pi\circ\pi^{\vdash}=\text{id}_{\mathfrak{R}}
, which shows that
\ell_{\mathfrak{R}}
is a join-preserving involution on
(\mathfrak{R},n)
.
To prove that
\ell_{\mathfrak{R}}
is anti-homomorphic, we use the anti-homomorphic property of
\ell
and again the surjectivity of
\pi
. Let
\beta_{1},\beta_{2}\in\mathfrak{R}
. Hence there exist
\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}\in\mathfrak{Q}
such that
\pi(\alpha_{i})=\beta_{i}

(i=1,2)
. Then:

\displaystyle\ell_{\mathfrak{R}}(\beta_{1}\ast\beta_{2})

\displaystyle=(\ell_{\mathfrak{R}}\circ\pi)(\alpha_{1}\ast\alpha_{2})=(\pi\circ\ell)(\alpha_{1}\ast\alpha_{2})=\pi(\ell(\alpha_{2}))\ast{\pi(\ell(\alpha_{1}))=\ell_{\mathfrak{R}}(\beta_{2})\ast\ell_{\mathfrak{R}}(\beta_{1})}.

Finally, (
2.1
) shows that
\pi
is an involutive quantale homomorphism w.r.t.
\ell_{\mathfrak{R}}
.
∎

.
First, we recall the construction of coequalizers in
\mathsf{Quant}
and explicitly define the respective nuclei
c_{0}
and
\overline{c}_{0}
associated with
\pi
and
\overline{\pi}
, respectively (cf.
[
3
, Thm. 2.2.6]
):

\displaystyle c_{0}

\displaystyle=\mathop{\textstyle\bigwedge}\{\,c\in\mathfrak{N}\mid c\circ j_{\mathfrak{Q}}\circ q_{\mathfrak{Q}}=c\circ j_{\mathfrak{R}}\circ q_{\mathfrak{R}}\,\},

\displaystyle\overline{c}_{0}

\displaystyle=\mathop{\textstyle\bigwedge}\{\,c\in\mathfrak{N}\mid c\circ j_{\mathfrak{Q}}\circ\vartheta_{\mathfrak{R}}\circ q_{\mathfrak{R}}=c\circ j_{\mathfrak{R}}\circ\vartheta_{\mathfrak{Q}}\circ q_{\mathfrak{Q}}\,\}

where the meet is computed pointwise. In particular, the following properties hold:

c_{0}\circ j_{\mathfrak{Q}}\circ q_{\mathfrak{Q}}=c_{0}\circ j_{\mathfrak{R}}\circ q_{\mathfrak{R}}\quad\text{and}\quad\overline{c}_{0}\circ j_{\mathfrak{Q}}\circ\vartheta_{\mathfrak{R}}\circ q_{\mathfrak{R}}=\overline{c}_{0}\circ j_{\mathfrak{R}}\circ\vartheta_{\mathfrak{Q}}\circ q_{\mathfrak{Q}}.

(a)
Necessity
: If
c_{0}
is involutive, then we derive the following relation from the commutativity of diagram (
2.2
):

\displaystyle c_{0}\circ j_{\mathfrak{Q}}\circ\vartheta_{\mathfrak{R}}\circ q_{\mathfrak{R}}

\displaystyle=c_{0}\circ\ell\circ j_{\mathfrak{R}}\circ q_{\mathfrak{R}}=\ell\circ c_{0}\circ j_{\mathfrak{R}}\circ q_{\mathfrak{R}}=\ell\circ c_{0}\circ j_{\mathfrak{Q}}\circ q_{\mathfrak{Q}}

\displaystyle=c_{0}\circ\ell\circ j_{\mathfrak{Q}}\circ q_{\mathfrak{Q}}=c_{0}\circ j_{\mathfrak{R}}\circ\vartheta_{\mathfrak{Q}}\circ q_{\mathfrak{Q}}.

Hence,
\overline{c}_{0}\leq c_{0}
follows. On the other hand, since

\ell\circ\overline{c}_{0}\circ\ell\in\mathfrak{N}
and

\displaystyle\ell\circ\overline{c}_{0}\circ\ell\circ j_{\mathfrak{Q}}\circ q_{\mathfrak{Q}}

\displaystyle=\ell\circ\overline{c}_{0}\circ j_{\mathfrak{R}}\circ\vartheta_{\mathfrak{Q}}\circ q_{\mathfrak{Q}}=\ell\circ\overline{c}_{0}\circ j_{\mathfrak{Q}}\circ\vartheta_{\mathfrak{R}}\circ q_{\mathfrak{R}}=\ell\circ\overline{c}_{0}\circ\ell\circ j_{\mathfrak{R}}\circ q_{\mathfrak{R}},

c_{0}\leq\ell\circ\overline{c}_{0}\circ\ell
follows. Finally, using again the property that
c_{0}
is involutive, we obtain

c_{0}=\ell\circ\ell\circ c_{0}=\ell\circ c_{0}\circ\ell\leq\ell\circ\ell\circ\overline{c}_{0}\circ\ell\circ\ell=\overline{c}_{0}.

Hence,
c_{0}=\overline{c}_{0}
follows — i.e. the regular quotient objects w.r.t.
\pi
and
\overline{\pi}
coincide, and so the coequalizers of
\mathfrak{S}\mathrel{\hbox to35.23pt{\vbox to25.47pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 14.61325pt\lower-12.43175pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.7pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{}{{}}{}

are isomorphic.

(b)
Sufficiency
: Assume
c_{0}=\overline{c}_{0}
. Referring again to the commutativity of diagram (
2.2
), we obtain

\ell\circ c_{0}\circ\ell\circ j_{\mathfrak{Q}}\circ q_{\mathfrak{Q}}=\ell\circ c_{0}\circ j_{\mathfrak{R}}\circ\vartheta_{\mathfrak{Q}}\circ q_{\mathfrak{Q}}=\ell\circ c_{0}\circ j_{\mathfrak{Q}}\circ\vartheta_{\mathfrak{R}}\circ q_{\mathfrak{R}}=\ell\circ c_{0}\circ\ell\circ j_{\mathfrak{R}}\circ q_{\mathfrak{R}}.

Since
\ell\circ c_{0}\circ\ell
is a nucleus, the relation
c_{0}\leq\ell\circ c_{0}\circ\ell
follows. Hence,
c_{0}
is involutive.
∎

Theorem 2.3
.

Let
(\mathfrak{Q},m)
and
(\mathfrak{R},n)
be balanced quantales, and let
\mathfrak{Q}\mathrel{\hbox to20.47pt{\vbox to16.87pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 7.23361pt\lower-5.01602pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.7pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{{}}{}

be quantale anti-homomorphisms satisfying the following conditions
:

(2.3)

\vartheta_{\mathfrak{R}}\circ\vartheta_{\mathfrak{Q}}=1_{\mathfrak{Q}}\quad\text{and}\quad\vartheta_{\mathfrak{Q}}\circ\vartheta_{\mathfrak{R}}=1_{\mathfrak{R}}.

If
\bigl(\mathfrak{Q}\otimes\mathfrak{R},m\otimes n)
is the tensor product of
(\mathfrak{Q},m)
and
(\mathfrak{R},n)
, then there exists a quantale anti-homomorphism
\mathfrak{Q}\otimes\mathfrak{R}\mathrel{\hbox to13.35pt{\vbox to14.89pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 3.67326pt\lower-5.01602pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.7pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}{}{}{{{}{}}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{}{{{}{}}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 7.33 0}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{5.65002pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 7.82 0)} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray=none,stroke-dashoffset=0.0pt} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap=round} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin=round} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -3.38 4.07 C -2.76 1.63 -1.38 0.47 0 0 C -1.38 -0.47 -2.76 -1.63 -3.38 -4.07}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{}{{

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{3.0pt}{-1.68301pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 4.15 -2.33)} \pgfsys@hbox{58}\lxSVG@closescope }}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-0.34026pt}{1.68301pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 -0.47 2.33)} \pgfsys@hbox{58}\lxSVG@closescope }}}

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\mathfrak{Q}\otimes\mathfrak{R}
determined by
:

(2.4)

\ell=c_{\mathfrak{R}\mathfrak{Q}}\circ(\vartheta_{\mathfrak{Q}}\otimes\vartheta_{\mathfrak{R}}).

Moreover,
\ell
satisfies the property
\ell\circ\ell=1_{\mathfrak{Q}\otimes\mathfrak{R}}
— i.e.
(\mathfrak{Q}\otimes\mathfrak{R},m\otimes n,\ell)

is an involutive quantale, and the following diagram is commutative
:

(2.5)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{Q}\otimes\mathfrak{R}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{Q}\otimes\mathfrak{R}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{Q}\otimes\mathfrak{R}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{Q}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{R}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{Q}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ell}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ell}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j_{\mathfrak{Q}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\vartheta_{\mathfrak{Q}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j_{\mathfrak{R}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\vartheta_{\mathfrak{R}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j_{\mathfrak{Q}}}

If
(\mathfrak{Q},m)
is left-sided,
(\mathfrak{R},n)
is right-sided, and both quantales are semi-unital, then the involution
\ell
on
\mathfrak{Q}\otimes\mathfrak{R}
is uniquely determined by the commutativity of the diagram, and an element
p\in\mathfrak{Q}\otimes\mathfrak{R}
is a hermitian prime element of
(\mathfrak{Q}\otimes\mathfrak{R},m\otimes n)
if and only if there exist a prime element
q
in
(\mathfrak{Q},m)
such that
p=(q\otimes\top)\vee(\top\otimes\vartheta_{\mathfrak{Q}}(q))
.

Proof.

To show that
\ell
defined by (
2.4
) is an involution on
(\mathfrak{Q}\otimes\mathfrak{R},m\otimes n)
, we recall that every tensor of
\mathfrak{Q}\otimes\mathfrak{R}
is an appropriate join of elementary tensors.
Hence, it is sufficient to verify the following properties:

(i)

\ell(\ell(\alpha\otimes\beta))=\alpha\otimes\beta
for each
\alpha\in\mathfrak{Q}
and
\beta\in\mathfrak{R}.

(ii)

\ell((\alpha_{1}\otimes\beta_{1})\star(\alpha_{2}\otimes\beta_{2}))=\ell(\alpha_{2}\otimes\beta_{2})\star\ell(\alpha_{1}\otimes\beta_{1})
for each
\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}\in\mathfrak{Q}
and
\beta_{1},\beta_{2}\in\mathfrak{R}.

For property (i), we have:

\ell(\ell(\alpha\otimes\beta))=\ell\bigl(\vartheta_{\mathfrak{R}}(\beta)\otimes\vartheta_{\mathfrak{Q}}(\alpha)\bigr)=\vartheta_{\mathfrak{R}}(\vartheta_{\mathfrak{Q}}(\alpha))\otimes\vartheta_{\mathfrak{Q}}(\vartheta_{\mathfrak{R}}(\beta))=\alpha\otimes\beta.

Since
\vartheta_{\mathfrak{Q}}
and
\vartheta_{\mathfrak{R}}
are quantale anti-homomorphisms, we obtain:

\displaystyle\ell((\alpha_{1}\otimes\beta_{1})\star(\alpha_{2}\otimes\beta_{2}))

\displaystyle=\ell((\alpha_{1}\ast\alpha_{2})\otimes(\beta_{1}\ast\beta_{2}))=\vartheta_{\mathfrak{R}}(\beta_{1}\ast\beta_{2})\otimes\vartheta_{\mathfrak{Q}}(\alpha_{1}\ast\alpha_{2})

\displaystyle=(\vartheta_{\mathfrak{R}}(\beta_{2})\ast\vartheta_{\mathfrak{R}}(\beta_{1}))\otimes(\vartheta_{\mathfrak{Q}}(\alpha_{2})\ast\vartheta_{\mathfrak{Q}}(\alpha_{1}))

\displaystyle=(\vartheta_{\mathfrak{R}}(\beta_{2})\otimes\vartheta_{\mathfrak{Q}}(\alpha_{2}))\star(\vartheta_{\mathfrak{R}}(\beta_{1})\otimes\vartheta_{\mathfrak{Q}}(\alpha_{1}))=\ell(\alpha_{2}\otimes\beta_{2})\star\ell(\alpha_{1}\otimes\beta_{1}).

Hence (ii) is verified.

Since (
2.3
) implies
\top=\vartheta_{\mathfrak{Q}}(\vartheta_{\mathfrak{R}}(\top))\leq\vartheta_{\mathfrak{Q}}(\top)
and
\top=\vartheta_{\mathfrak{R}}(\vartheta_{\mathfrak{Q}}(\top))\leq\vartheta_{\mathfrak{R}}(\top)
—
i.e.
\vartheta_{\mathfrak{Q}}
and
\vartheta_{\mathfrak{R}}
preserve the respective universal upper bounds, we obtain for all

\alpha\in\mathfrak{Q}

\ell(j_{\mathfrak{Q}}(\alpha))=\ell(\alpha\otimes\top)=\top\otimes\vartheta_{\mathfrak{Q}}(\alpha)=(j_{\mathfrak{R}}\circ\vartheta_{\mathfrak{Q}})(\alpha),

Hence,
\ell\circ j_{\mathfrak{Q}}=j_{\mathfrak{R}}\circ\vartheta_{\mathfrak{Q}}
follows.
Analogously, we verify
\ell\circ j_{\mathfrak{R}}=j_{\mathfrak{Q}}\circ\vartheta_{\mathfrak{R}}
, and so the diagram (
2.5
) is commutative.

Finally, if
(\mathfrak{Q},m)
is left-sided and semi-unital, it follows that
\top
is a left-unit in
\mathfrak{Q}
; respectively, if
(\mathfrak{R},n)
is right-sided and semi-unital, then
\top
is a right-unit in
\mathfrak{R}
. Consequently the commutativity of the diagram (
2.5
) implies:

\displaystyle\ell(\alpha\otimes\beta)

\displaystyle=\ell((\top\ast\alpha)\otimes(\beta\ast\top))=\ell(j_{\mathfrak{R}}(\beta)\star j_{\mathfrak{Q}}(\alpha))=\ell(j_{\mathfrak{Q}}(\alpha))\star\ell(j_{\mathfrak{R}}(\beta))

\displaystyle=j_{\mathfrak{R}}(\vartheta_{\mathfrak{Q}}(\alpha))\star j_{\mathfrak{Q}}(\vartheta_{\mathfrak{R}}(\beta))=(\top\otimes\vartheta_{\mathfrak{Q}}(\alpha))\star(\vartheta_{\mathfrak{R}}(\beta)\otimes\top)=\vartheta_{\mathfrak{R}}(\beta)\otimes\vartheta_{\mathfrak{Q}}(\alpha)

for each
\alpha\in\mathfrak{Q}
and
\beta\in\mathfrak{R}
.
Again, since every tensor of
\mathfrak{Q}\otimes\mathfrak{R}
is a join of elementary tensors, the formula (
2.4
) follows.
Finally, it follows from Theorem
1.9
that
p\in\sigma(\mathfrak{Q}\otimes\mathfrak{R})
if and only if there exist
q\in\sigma(\mathfrak{Q})
and
r\in\sigma(\mathfrak{R})
such that
p=(q\otimes\top)\vee(\top\otimes r)
. Since

\ell(p)=\ell(q\otimes\top)\vee\ell(\top\otimes r)=(\top\otimes\vartheta_{\mathfrak{Q}}(q))\vee(\vartheta_{\mathfrak{R}}(r)\otimes\top),

it follows that
p
is hermitian if and only if
\vartheta_{\mathfrak{Q}}(q)=r
and
\vartheta_{\mathfrak{R}}(r)=q
.
∎

.

.

If

with
\iota\not\in\{\,\bot,\top\,\}
, then

is still an embedding from

into

, but not a strong quantale homomorphism.

Corollary 2.4
.

Let
\mathfrak{Q}
be an involutive and semi-unital quantale. Further, let
(\mathds{L}(\mathfrak{Q}),m_{l})
and
(\mathds{R}(\mathfrak{Q}),m_{r})
be the subquantales of all left-sided elements and right-sided elements of
\mathfrak{Q}
, respectively.
Then there exists a unique involution
\ell
on the tensor product
(\mathds{L}(\mathfrak{Q})\otimes\mathds{R}(\mathfrak{Q}),m_{l}\otimes m_{r})
satisfying the following property
:

(2.6)

\ell(\alpha\otimes\beta)=\beta^{\prime}\otimes\alpha^{\prime},\qquad\alpha\in\mathds{L}(\mathfrak{Q}),\,\beta\in\mathds{R}(\mathfrak{Q}).

Proof.

The restriction of the involution in
\mathfrak{Q}
induces a pair of anti-homomorphisms between
\mathds{L}(\mathfrak{Q})
and
\mathds{R}(\mathfrak{Q})
satisfying (
2.3
). Since
\mathds{L}(\mathfrak{Q})
and
\mathds{R}(\mathfrak{Q})
are semi-unital, we conclude from Theorem
2.3
that there exists a unique involution
\ell
on the tensor product
\mathds{L}(\mathfrak{Q})\otimes\mathds{R}(\mathfrak{Q})
satisfying (
2.6
).
∎

2.1.
Tensorially involutive quantale associated with an involutive and semi-unital quantale

The previous Corollary
2.4
suggests to introduce the following terminology. Let
\mathfrak{Q}
be an involutive and semi-unital quantale. Then the involutive quantale
(\mathds{L}(\mathfrak{Q})\otimes\mathds{R}(\mathfrak{Q}),m_{l}\otimes m_{r},\ell)
satisfying (
2.6
) is called the
tensorially involutive
quantale associated with
\mathfrak{Q}
.

Remark 2.5
.

The tensorially involutive quantale
(\mathds{L}(\mathfrak{Q})\otimes\mathds{R}(\mathfrak{Q}),m_{l}\otimes m_{r},\ell)
of
\mathfrak{Q}
is always semi-unital and semi-integral. Moreover, it is pre-idempotent if and only if
\mathds{L}(\mathfrak{Q})
is idempotent and it is bisymmetric if and only if
\mathds{L}(\mathfrak{Q})
is bisymmetric.

The next result describes the hermitian spectrum of
(\mathds{L}(\mathfrak{Q})\otimes\mathds{R}(\mathfrak{Q}),m_{l}\otimes m_{r},\ell)
in terms of the spectrum of
\mathds{L}(\mathfrak{Q})
.

Corollary 2.6
.

Let
\mathfrak{Q}
be an involutive and semi-unital quantale, and let
(\mathds{L}(\mathfrak{Q})\otimes\mathds{R}(\mathfrak{Q}),m_{l}\otimes m_{r},\ell)
be its tensorially involutive quantale. Then there exists a bijective map

{\sigma(\mathds{L}(\mathfrak{Q}))\mathrel{\hbox to18.19pt{\vbox to16.25pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 6.0963pt\lower-5.01602pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.7pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}{}{}{{{}{}}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{}{{{}{}}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 7.33 0}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{5.65002pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 7.82 0)} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray=none,stroke-dashoffset=0.0pt} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap=round} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin=round} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -3.38 4.07 C -2.76 1.63 -1.38 0.47 0 0 C -1.38 -0.47 -2.76 -1.63 -3.38 -4.07}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{3.0pt}{-1.68301pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 4.15 -2.33)} \pgfsys@hbox{58}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-2.76329pt}{3.04411pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 -3.82 4.21)} \pgfsys@hbox{58}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\sigma_{h}(\mathds{L}(\mathfrak{Q})\otimes\mathds{R}(\mathfrak{Q}))}

given by

\psi_{l}(q)=(q\otimes\top)\vee(\top\otimes q^{\prime})
for each
q\in\sigma(\mathds{L}(\mathfrak{Q}))
.

Proof.

Since every hermitian prime element
p
of
(\mathds{L}(\mathfrak{Q})\otimes\mathds{R}(\mathfrak{Q}),m_{l}\otimes m_{r},\ell)
has the form
p=(q\otimes\top)\vee(\top\otimes q^{\prime})
with
q\in\sigma(\mathds{L}(\mathfrak{Q}))
(cf. Theorem
2.3
), the map
\psi_{l}
is surjective. The injectivity of this map follows from the subsequent equivalence for all
\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}\in\mathds{L}(\mathfrak{Q})
and
\beta_{1},\beta_{2}\in\mathds{R}(\mathfrak{Q})
:

(\alpha_{1}\otimes\top)\vee(\top\otimes\beta_{1})=(\alpha_{2}\otimes\top)\vee(\top\otimes\beta_{2})\iff(\alpha_{1}=\alpha_{2}\text{ and }\beta_{1}=\beta_{2}).\qed

2.2.
The quantization of
\boldsymbol{2}

Let
\mathfrak{Q}
be an involutive and semi-unital quantale. Obviously, if
\mathds{L}(\mathfrak{Q})=\mathds{R}(\mathfrak{Q})=\mathds{I}(\mathfrak{Q})=\{\,\bot,\top\,\}
, then the tensorially involutive quantale associated with
\mathfrak{Q}
is the Boolean
2
-chain
\boldsymbol{2}
.
We now present the simplest example of a tensorially involutive quantale associated with a quantale
\mathfrak{Q}
that has some left-sided element being different from
\bot
and
\top
.

First, we recall that on the
3
–chain
C_{3}=\{\,\bot,a,\top\,\}
with
\bot<a<\top
,
there exist exactly two semi-unital and non-commutative quantales, namely
C_{3}^{\ell}=(C_{3},m_{\ell})

and
C_{3}^{r}=(C_{3},m_{r})
, where
a\in C_{3}^{\ell}
is left-sided, but not right-sided and
a\in C_{3}^{r}
is right-sided,
but not left-sided. In particular, the multiplication tables of the quantale multiplications
\ast_{\ell}
in
C_{3}^{\ell}
and
\ast_{r}
in
C_{3}^{r}
are given by:

\ast_{\ell}

a

\top

a

a

\top

\top

a

\top

\ast_{r}

a

\top

a

a

a

\top

\top

\top

Now we consider an involutive and semi-unital quantale
\mathfrak{Q}
such that
\mathds{I}(\mathfrak{Q})=\{\,\bot,\top\,\}

and the subquantales
\mathds{L}(\mathfrak{Q})
and
\mathds{R}(\mathfrak{Q})
are isomorphic to
C_{3}^{\ell}
and
C_{3}^{r}
.
Then the tensorially involutive quantale associated with
\mathfrak{Q}
is isomorphic to the tensor product
C_{3}^{\ell}\otimes C_{3}^{r}
.
Since
C_{3}^{\ell}
and
C_{3}^{r}
are idempotent and therefore bisymmetric, the quantale
C_{3}^{\ell}\otimes C_{3}^{r}
is involutive, semi-unital, semi-integral, pre-idempotent and bisymmetric (cf. Remark
2.5
).
To simplify the notation we will denote
C_{3}^{\ell}\otimes C_{3}^{r}
by
\mathfrak{Q}_{\boldsymbol{2}}
, and call it the
quantization of
\boldsymbol{2}
.

Since
\mathfrak{Q}_{\boldsymbol{2}}
consists of six elements, we introduce the following abbreviations:

a_{\ell}:=a\otimes\top,\quad a_{r}:=\top\otimes a,\quad b:=a\otimes a,\quad c:=a_{\ell}\vee a_{r}.

Then the action of the involution on
\mathfrak{Q}_{\boldsymbol{2}}
is given by
\top^{\prime}=\top
,
c^{\prime}=c
,
a_{\ell}^{\prime}=a_{r}
,
a_{r}^{\prime}=a_{\ell}
,
b^{\prime}=b
, and
\bot^{\prime}=\bot
, and the Hasse diagram and the multiplication table of
\mathfrak{Q}_{\boldsymbol{2}}
have the form:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\top}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{a_{\ell}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{a_{r}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bot}

\star

b

a_{\ell}

a_{r}

\top

It is interesting to see that the right quantale multiplication in
\mathfrak{Q}_{\boldsymbol{2}}
satisfies the following properties:

(2.7)

\underline{\mbox{\,\,\,\,}}\star b=\underline{\mbox{\,\,\,\,}}\star a_{\ell}\quad\text{and}\quad\underline{\mbox{\,\,\,\,}}\star a_{r}=\underline{\mbox{\,\,\,\,}}\star c=\underline{\mbox{\,\,\,\,}}\star\top.

Since
\mathfrak{Q}_{\boldsymbol{2}}
is involutive, we also have the respective properties for the left quantale multiplication:

b\star\underline{\mbox{\,\,\,\,}}=a_{r}\star\underline{\mbox{\,\,\,\,}}\quad\text{and}\quad a_{\ell}\star\underline{\mbox{\,\,\,\,}}=c\star\underline{\mbox{\,\,\,\,}}=\top\star\underline{\mbox{\,\,\,\,}}.

Referring to Lemma
1.1
, the quantale multiplication in
\mathfrak{Q}_{\boldsymbol{2}}
satisfies the subsequent
important property for all
\alpha,\beta\in\mathfrak{Q}_{\boldsymbol{2}}
:

(2.8)

{\alpha\star\gamma\star\beta=\alpha\star\beta\qquad\gamma\in\mathfrak{Q}_{\boldsymbol{2}}\setminus\{\bot\}}.

Finally, the element
c=a_{\ell}\vee a_{r}
is the largest prime element of
\mathfrak{Q}_{\boldsymbol{2}}
. Obviously,
c
is also a hermitian prime element.

The importance of the quantization of
\boldsymbol{2}
is explained in the next remark.

. If

is also involutive, then

is involutive if and only if

is hermitian.

can be identified with the strong quantale homomorphism

.

is a hermitian prime element of

,

is hermitian for every involutive quantale homomorphism. Hence, the final assertion follows from (

).
∎

2.3.
Quantic frames

Let
\mathsf{BSQuant}
be the category of balanced and bisymmetric quantales and strong quantale homomorphisms. We now refer to
[
3
, Def. 2.5.13]
and recall that quantic frames are special objects of
\mathsf{BSQuant}
. More precisely, an involutive, bisymmetric and semi-unital quantale
\mathfrak{Q}=(\mathfrak{Q},m,\ell)
is called a
quantic frame
if
\mathfrak{Q}
satisfies the following additional properties:

(A)

Every left-sided (resp. right-sided) element is idempotent.

(B)

Every two-sided element is hermitian.

(C)

Let
{\mathds{I}(\mathfrak{Q})\xhookrightarrow{\,q_{\mathds{L}(\mathfrak{Q})}\,}\mathds{L}(\mathfrak{Q})}
,
\mathds{I}(\mathfrak{Q})\xhookrightarrow{\,q_{\mathds{R}(\mathfrak{Q})}\,}\mathds{R}(\mathfrak{Q})
,
\mathds{L}(\mathfrak{Q})\xhookrightarrow{\,\iota_{\mathds{L}(\mathfrak{Q})}\,}\mathfrak{Q}
and
\mathds{R}(\mathfrak{Q})\xhookrightarrow{\,\iota_{\mathds{R}(\mathfrak{Q})}\,}\mathfrak{Q}
be the inclusion arrows.
Then the commutative diagram

(2.10)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{Q}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathds{L}(\mathfrak{Q})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathds{R}(\mathfrak{Q})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathds{I}(\mathfrak{Q})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{\mathds{L}(\mathfrak{Q})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{\mathds{L}(\mathfrak{Q})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{\mathds{L}(\mathfrak{Q})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{\mathds{R}(\mathfrak{Q})}}

is a pushout square in
\mathsf{BSQuant}
.

Before we proceed, we first recall a characterization of condition
(C)
by a well known coequalizer condition. Since the tensorially involutive quantale
\mathds{L}(\mathfrak{Q})\otimes\mathds{R}(\mathfrak{Q})
associated with a semi-unital, bisymmetric and involutive quantale
\mathfrak{Q}
is the coproduct of
\mathds{L}(\mathfrak{Q})
and
\mathds{R}(\mathfrak{Q})
in
\mathsf{BSQuant}
with the coprojections

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\mathfrak{Q}

by
\pi:=\iota_{\mathds{L}(\mathfrak{Q})}\sqcup\iota_{\mathds{R}(\mathfrak{Q})}
.

Let
\mathfrak{Q}
an involutive and semi-unital such that
\mathds{I}(\mathfrak{Q})=\{\,\bot,\top\,\}
— i.e.
\mathfrak{Q}
is a factor.

(a)

If
\mathfrak{Q}
is a quantic frame, then
\pi
is an isomorphism — i.e.
\mathfrak{Q}
is isomorphic to its tensorially involutive quantale.

(b)

If
\mathds{L}(\mathfrak{Q})
is idempotent and therefore bisymmetric, then
\mathds{L}(\mathfrak{Q})\otimes\mathds{R}(\mathfrak{Q})
is a quantic frame (cf. Remark
2.5
). Consequently,
\mathfrak{Q}_{\boldsymbol{2}}
is the simplest
non-trivial
example of a quantic frame.

Finally, we recall some general properties of quantic frames. First, we emphasize again that every quantic frame is bisymmetric. Further, it follows from
(A)
and
(C)
that every quantic frame is semi-unital, semi-integral and pre-idempotent. A quantic frame is commutative if and only if it is a traditional frame (cf.
[
3
, p. 149]
and
[
10
]
).

Proposition 2.10
.

Let
\mathfrak{Q}
be a quantic frame.

(a)

If
\mathds{I}(\mathfrak{Q})
does not have zero divisors, then for
\alpha\in\mathds{L}(\mathfrak{Q})\setminus\{\bot\}
and for
\beta\in\mathds{R}(\mathfrak{Q})\setminus\{\bot\}
the relation
\pi(\alpha\otimes\beta)\neq\bot
holds.

(b)

\pi
is involutive.

(c)

The restriction of the right adjoint map
\pi^{\vdash}
of
\pi
to
\sigma_{h}(\mathfrak{Q})
is a bijective map from
\sigma_{h}(\mathfrak{Q})
to
\sigma_{h}(\mathds{L}(\mathfrak{Q})\otimes\mathds{R}(\mathfrak{Q}))
.

(d)

The restriction of
\pi
to
\sigma_{h}(\mathds{L}(\mathfrak{Q})\otimes\mathds{R}(\mathfrak{Q}))
is a bijective map from
\sigma_{h}(\mathds{L}(\mathfrak{Q})\otimes\mathds{R}(\mathfrak{Q}))
to
\sigma_{h}(\mathfrak{Q})
.

Proof.

(a) We choose
\alpha\in\mathds{L}(\mathfrak{Q})
and
\beta\in\mathds{R}(\mathfrak{Q})
with
\pi(\alpha\otimes\beta)=\bot
. Then
\alpha\ast\top
and
\top\ast\beta
are two-sided and since
\iota_{\mathds{L}(\mathfrak{Q})}=\pi\circ j_{\mathds{L}(\mathfrak{Q})}
and
\iota_{\mathds{R}(\mathfrak{Q})}=\pi\circ j_{\mathds{R}(\mathfrak{Q})}
, we obtain:

\displaystyle\top\ast\beta\ast\alpha\ast\top

\displaystyle=(\pi\circ j_{\mathds{R}(\mathfrak{Q})})(\top\ast\beta)\ast(\pi\circ j_{\mathds{L}(\mathfrak{Q})})(\alpha\ast\top)=\pi((\top\otimes(\top\ast\beta))\star((\alpha\ast\top)\otimes\top))

\displaystyle=\pi((\top\otimes\top)\star(\alpha\otimes\beta)\star(\top\otimes\top))=\pi(\top\otimes\top)\ast\pi(\alpha\otimes\beta)\ast\pi(\top\otimes\top)=\bot,

hence
\top\ast\beta=\bot
or
\alpha\ast\top=\bot
holds. Since
\mathfrak{Q}
is semi-unital,
\alpha=\bot
or
\beta=\bot
follows.

(b) Let
\alpha\in\mathds{L}(\mathfrak{Q})
and
\beta\in\mathds{R}(\mathfrak{Q})
. Since
\pi(\alpha\otimes\top)=\alpha
,
\pi(\top\otimes\beta)=\beta
,
\pi(\beta^{\prime}\otimes\top)=\beta^{\prime}
,
\pi(\top\otimes\alpha^{\prime})=\alpha^{\prime}
, and
\mathds{L}(\mathfrak{Q})\otimes\mathds{R}(\mathfrak{Q})
is the tensorially involutive quantale associated with
\mathfrak{Q}
, we refer to Corollary
2.4
and obtain:

\displaystyle\pi(\alpha\otimes\beta)^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\pi((\top\otimes\beta)\ast(\alpha\otimes\top))^{\prime}=(\pi(\top\otimes\beta)\ast\pi(\alpha\otimes\top))^{\prime}=(\beta\ast\alpha)^{\prime}=\alpha^{\prime}\ast\beta^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\pi(\top\otimes\alpha^{\prime})\ast\pi(\beta^{\prime}\otimes\top)=\pi((\top\otimes\alpha^{\prime})\ast(\beta^{\prime}\otimes\top))=\pi(\beta^{\prime}\otimes\alpha^{\prime})=\pi(\ell(\alpha\otimes\beta)).

(c) Since
\pi
is surjective and involutive (cf. (b)), the right adjoint map
{\mathfrak{Q}}\mathrel{\hbox to19.55pt{\vbox to16.52pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 6.77531pt\lower-5.01602pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.7pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{{}}{}

}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-1.00478pt}{1.68301pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 -1.39 2.33)} \pgfsys@hbox{58}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\mathfrak{Q}
, and consequently, there exists a unique strong quantale homomorphism
\mathfrak{Q}\mathrel{\hbox to18.58pt{\vbox to15.89pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 6.29192pt\lower-5.01602pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.7pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}{}{}{{{}{}}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{}{{{}{}}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 7.33 0}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{5.65002pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 7.82 0)} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray=none,stroke-dashoffset=0.0pt} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap=round} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin=round} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -3.38 4.07 C -2.76 1.63 -1.38 0.47 0 0 C -1.38 -0.47 -2.76 -1.63 -3.38 -4.07}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{3.0pt}{-1.68301pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 4.15 -2.33)} \pgfsys@hbox{58}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-2.95891pt}{2.68301pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 -4.09 3.71)} \pgfsys@hbox{58}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\mathfrak{Q}_{\boldsymbol{2}}
such that
h_{p}=h_{0}\circ\pi
. Since
h_{p}
and
\pi
are involutive, so is
h_{0}
, and
p_{0}:=h_{0}^{\vdash}(c)\in\sigma_{h}(\mathfrak{Q})
holds. Hence,
p=h_{p}^{\vdash}(c)=\pi^{\vdash}(h_{0}^{\vdash}(c))=\pi^{\vdash}(p_{0})
follows,
and the image
of the restriction of
\pi^{\vdash}
to
\sigma_{h}(\mathfrak{Q})
coincides with
\sigma_{h}(\mathds{L}(\mathfrak{Q})\otimes\mathds{R}(\mathfrak{Q}))
— i.e. (c) is verified.

(d) Being a coequalizer,
\pi
is necessarily surjective, and the relation
\pi\circ\pi^{\vdash}=1_{\mathfrak{Q}}
holds. Hence, we conclude from (c) that it is sufficient to show that the restriction of
\pi
to
\sigma_{h}(\mathds{L}(\mathfrak{Q})\otimes\mathds{R}(\mathfrak{Q}))
is injective. Therefore, let
p\in\sigma_{h}(\mathds{L}(\mathfrak{Q})\otimes\mathds{R}(\mathfrak{Q}))
. With regard to (c) we can choose
r\in\sigma_{h}(\mathfrak{Q})
with
p=\pi^{\vdash}(r)
. Then we obtain

\pi^{\vdash}(\pi(p))=\pi^{\vdash}(r)=p
.
Hence (d) follows.
∎

Corollary 2.11
.

Let
\mathfrak{Q}
be a quantic frame. Then
there exists a bijective map
\sigma(\mathds{L}(\mathfrak{Q}))\mathrel{\hbox to15.26pt{\vbox to14.41pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 4.6295pt\lower-5.01602pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.7pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}{}{}{{{}{}}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{}{{{}{}}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 7.33 0}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{5.65002pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 7.82 0)} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray=none,stroke-dashoffset=0.0pt} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap=round} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin=round} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -3.38 4.07 C -2.76 1.63 -1.38 0.47 0 0 C -1.38 -0.47 -2.76 -1.63 -3.38 -4.07}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{3.0pt}{-1.68301pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 4.15 -2.33)} \pgfsys@hbox{58}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{}{{
