{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-1.2965pt}{3.0441pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 -1.79 4.21)} \pgfsys@hbox{58}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\sigma_{h}(\mathfrak{Q})
given by

\varphi(q)=q\vee q^{\prime}
for each
q\in\sigma(\mathds{L}(\mathfrak{Q}))
.

Proof.

Since
\iota_{\mathds{L}(\mathfrak{Q})}=\pi\circ j_{\mathds{L}(\mathfrak{Q})}
and
\iota_{\mathds{R}(\mathfrak{Q})}=\pi\circ j_{\mathds{R}(\mathfrak{Q})}
, the assertion follows from Corollary
2.6
and Proposition
2.10
(d).
∎

Proposition 2.12
.

A quantic frame
\mathfrak{Q}
does not have zero divisors if and only if
\mathds{I}(\mathfrak{Q})
does not have zero divisors.

Proof.

The necessity is obvious. To verify the sufficiency we choose
\alpha_{0},\beta_{0}\in\mathfrak{Q}
with
\alpha_{0}\ast\beta_{0}=\bot
and assume
\alpha_{0}\neq\bot
and
\beta_{0}\neq\bot
. Being a coequalizer,
\pi
is necessarily surjective, and we now choose elementary tensors
\alpha_{1}\otimes\alpha_{2},\,\beta_{1}\otimes\beta_{2}\in\mathds{L}(\mathfrak{Q})\otimes\mathds{R}(\mathfrak{Q})
such that

\bot\neq\pi(\alpha_{1}\otimes\alpha_{2})\leq\alpha_{0}\quad\text{and}\quad\bot\neq\pi(\beta_{1}\otimes\beta_{2})\leq\beta_{0}

hold. Hence
\alpha_{1}\neq\bot,\,\beta_{1}\neq\bot,\,\alpha_{2}\neq\bot
and
\beta_{2}\neq\bot
. Since
\mathds{I}(\mathfrak{Q})
does not have zero divisors, we conclude from
Lemma
1.2
(a) that
\alpha_{1}\ast\beta_{1}\neq\bot
and
\alpha_{2}\ast\beta_{2}\neq\bot
. Now we apply Proposition
2.10
(a) and obtain

\pi\bigl((\alpha_{1}\ast\beta_{1})\otimes(\alpha_{2}\ast\beta_{2})\bigr)\neq\bot
. Thus

\bot\neq\pi\bigl((\alpha_{1}\ast\beta_{1})\otimes(\alpha_{2}\ast\beta_{2})\bigr)=\pi(\alpha_{1}\otimes\alpha_{2})\ast\pi(\beta_{1}\otimes\beta_{2})\leq\alpha_{0}\ast\beta_{0}

follows, which is a contradiction. Hence the assumption is false and the assertion is verified.
∎

2.4.
The spectrum of a unital
C^{*}
-algebra

The next example shows that every unital
C^{*}
-algebra
A
gives rise to an involutive quantale playing the role of the spectrum of
A
.

Example 2.13
.

Let
A=(A,+,\cdot,\mbox{}^{*},1)
be a unital
C^{*}
-algebra with unit
1
and involution
\mbox{}^{*}
. Following
[
16
]
, we consider the involutive and unital quantale
\mathds{M}axA
of all closed linear subspaces of
A
provided with the following quantale multiplication:

M_{1}\ast M_{2}=\text{top.\ closure}\bigl(\bigl\{\,\mathop{\textstyle\sum}\limits_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\cdot b_{i}\mid n\in\mathds{N},\,a_{i}\in M_{1},\,b_{i}\in M_{2}\,\bigr\}\bigr),\qquad M_{1},M_{2}\in\mathds{M}axA.

The involution of
\mathds{M}axA
assigns to any closed linear subspace
M
the adjoint closed linear subspace
M^{\prime}
determined by

M^{\prime}=\{\,a^{*}\in A\mid a\in M\,\},

where
\mbox{}^{*}
is the involution of the given
C^{*}
-algebra
A
.

The subquantale
\mathds{L}(\mathds{M}axA)
of all left-sided elements of
\mathds{M}axA
coincides with the quantale
\mathds{L}(A)
of all closed left ideals of
A
, and the restriction of the quantale multiplication
\ast
to
\mathds{L}(A)
is the usual
ideal multiplication
. The same applies to right-sided elements of
\mathds{M}axA
— i.e.
\mathds{R}(\mathds{M}axA)=\mathds{R}(A)
is the quantale of all closed right ideals of
A
and again the restriction of
\ast
is the usual ideal multiplication.

The extension of the quantale multiplication
\ast
to a semigroup operation in
\mathsf{Sup}
is denoted by
m
, and consequently, the extension of the ideal multiplication in
\mathds{L}(A)
and
\mathds{R}(A)
is denoted by
m_{\mathds{L}}
in
\mathds{L}(A)
and
m_{\mathds{R}}
in
\mathds{R}(A)
, respectively. Since
\mathds{M}axA
is unital,
(\mathds{L}(A),m_{\mathds{L}})
and
(\mathds{R}(A),m_{\mathds{R}})
are semi-unital quantales.
In particular, the universal upper bound
\top=A
is a left unit in
\mathds{L}(A)
and a right unit in
\mathds{R}(A)
, and consequently,
(\mathds{L}(A),m_{\mathds{L}})
and
(\mathds{R}(A),m_{\mathds{R}})
are semi-integral. Therefore, we can apply Fact 1 and conclude that maximal elements of
\mathds{L}(A)
and
\mathds{R}(A)
, respectively, are strongly prime, since
(\mathds{L}(A),m_{\mathds{L}})
and
(\mathds{R}(A),m_{\mathds{R}})
are semi-unital. Now we use the fundamental property of
C^{*}
-algebras that every closed left (resp. right) ideal of
A
is an intersection of maximal left (resp. right) ideals of
A
(cf.
[
13
, 10.2.10 Thm. (iii)]
). Hence the semi-unital quantales
(\mathds{L}(A),m_{\mathds{L}})
and
(\mathds{R}(A),m_{\mathds{R}})
are strongly spatial, and Proposition
1.5
implies that the ideal multiplication in
\mathds{L}(A)
and
\mathds{R}(A)
is bisymmetric and idempotent (cf. Proposition
1.5
(iii)).

With regard to the previous observations, we conclude that the tensorially involutive quantale
(\mathds{L}(A)\otimes\mathds{R}(A),m_{\mathds{L}}\otimes m_{\mathds{R}},\ell)
associated with
\mathds{M}axA
is semi-unital, semi-integral, pre-idempotent and bisymmetric (cf. Remark
2.5
). Since the one-to-one relationship between pure states of
A
and maximal left ideals is given by the construction of left kernels (cf.
[
13
, Thm. 10.2.10 (i) and (ii)]
),
we conclude from Corollary
2.6
that every pure state of
A
can be identified with a hermitian
prime element of the tensorially involutive quantale associated with
\mathds{M}axA
.

When the
C^{*}
-algebra
A
is commutative, the quantale
\mathds{M}axA
differs from the quantale of closed ideals of
A
. Hence, the quantale
\mathds{M}axA
is inappropriate in order to play the role of a spectrum of a
C^{*}
-algebra
A
. Therefore, we deviate from the approach taken by Mulvey and Pelletier and construct the spectrum of a
C^{*}
-algebra as follows.

In the first step, we consider the quantale
\mathds{I}(A)
of all closed two-sided ideals of
A
provided with the ideal multiplication. Then the universal upper bound
\top=A
is the unit with respect to the ideal multiplication, and consequently,
\mathds{I}(A)
is a frame, and the ideal multiplication coincides with the binary meet operation (cf.
[
3
, p. 120]
).

Moreover, if
\mathds{I}(A)\otimes\mathds{I}(A)\mathrel{\hbox to17.1pt{\vbox to13.05pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 5.54768pt\lower-5.01602pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.7pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}{}{}{{{}{}}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{}{{{}{}}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 7.33 0}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{5.65002pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 7.82 0)} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray=none,stroke-dashoffset=0.0pt} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap=round} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin=round} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -3.38 4.07 C -2.76 1.63 -1.38 0.47 0 0 C -1.38 -0.47 -2.76 -1.63 -3.38 -4.07}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{3.0pt}{-1.68301pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 4.15 -2.33)} \pgfsys@hbox{58}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-2.21468pt}{1.68301pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 -3.06 2.33)} \pgfsys@hbox{58}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\mathds{I}(A)
is now the semigroup operation in the sense of
\mathsf{Sup}
corresponding to the binary meet in
\mathds{I}(A)
, then
m
is commutative and even a quantale homomorphism

(\mathds{I}(A)\otimes\mathds{I}(A),m\otimes m)\mathrel{\hbox to17.1pt{\vbox to13.05pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 5.54768pt\lower-5.01602pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.7pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}{}{}{{{}{}}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{}{{{}{}}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 7.33 0}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{5.65002pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 7.82 0)} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray=none,stroke-dashoffset=0.0pt} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap=round} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin=round} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -3.38 4.07 C -2.76 1.63 -1.38 0.47 0 0 C -1.38 -0.47 -2.76 -1.63 -3.38 -4.07}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{3.0pt}{-1.68301pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 4.15 -2.33)} \pgfsys@hbox{58}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-2.21468pt}{1.68301pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 -3.06 2.33)} \pgfsys@hbox{58}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}(\mathds{I}(A),m).

In fact, if
I_{1}
,
I_{2}
,
I_{3}
and
I_{4}
are closed two-sided ideals of
A
, then the following relation holds for elementary tensors:

m((I_{1}\otimes I_{2})\star(I_{3}\otimes I_{4}))=m((I_{1}\wedge I_{3})\otimes(I_{2}\wedge I_{4}))=I_{1}\wedge I_{3}\wedge I_{2}\wedge I_{4}=m(I_{1}\otimes I_{2})\wedge m(I_{3}\otimes I_{4}).

In a second step, we refer to the inclusion maps
\mathds{I}(A)\xhookrightarrow{\,q_{\mathds{L}(A)}\,}\mathds{L}(A)
and
\mathds{I}(A)\xhookrightarrow{\,q_{\mathds{R}(A)}\,}\mathds{R}(A)
and consider the coequalizer

in the sense of

as the

. Hence, the
spectrum
of
A
is given by
(\mathds{V},m_{\mathds{V}})
.

Before we proceed, we explore some properties of the spectrum of
A
.

First, we notice that the domain of
\pi
is precisely the tensorially involutive quantale associated with
\mathds{M}axA
. Hence, the spectrum of
A
is a semi-unital, semi-integral, pre-idempotent and bisymmetric quantale. Moreover, since every closed two-sided ideal is self-adjoint (cf.
[
12
, p. 239 and 4.2.10 Cor.]
), the inclusion maps

\mathds{I}(A)\xhookrightarrow{\,q_{\mathds{L}(A)}\,}\mathds{L}(A)
and
\mathds{I}(A)\xhookrightarrow{\,q_{\mathds{R}(A)}\,}\mathds{R}(A)
satisfy the property:

\vartheta_{\mathds{L}(A)}\circ q_{\mathds{L}(A)}=q_{\mathds{R}(A)}\quad\text{and}\quad\vartheta_{\mathds{R}(A)}\circ q_{\mathds{R}(A)}=q_{\mathds{L}(A)}.

Then we conclude from Theorem
2.2
that the nucleus associated with
\pi
is involutive, and so Proposition
2.1
implies that there exists an involution on the spectrum of

turning the coequalizer
\pi
into an involutive quantale epimomorphism. Thus, the spectrum of
A
is in fact an involutive quantale
(\mathds{V},m_{\mathds{V}},\ell_{\mathds{V}})
, which we will denote by
\Omega_{q}(A)
(cf.
[
8
, Rem. 5.3]
).

is a quantale homomorphism with
k\circ j_{\mathds{L}(A)}=k\circ j_{\mathds{R}(A)}
, then every quantale homomorphism
\varphi
making the diagram

(2.11)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathds{I}(A)\otimes\mathds{I}(A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathds{I}(A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathds{D}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}

commutative has the form
\varphi=k\circ j_{\mathds{L}(A)}\,(=k\circ j_{\mathds{R}(A)})
. Obviously, the commutativity of (
2.11
) implies for all
I\in\mathds{I}(A)
:

k(j_{\mathds{L}(A)}(I))=\varphi(m(j_{\mathds{L}(A)}(I)))=\varphi(m(I\otimes A))=\varphi(I\ast A)=\varphi(I),

i.e.
\varphi
is uniquely determined by
k
.
On the other hand, in order to verify the existence of
\varphi
, we define
\varphi:=k\circ j_{\mathds{L}(A)}
and observe that for elementary tensors
I\otimes J\in\mathds{I}(A)\otimes\mathds{I}(A)
the following relation holds:

\displaystyle k(I\otimes J)

\displaystyle=k((A\otimes J)\star(I\otimes A))=k(j_{\mathds{R}(A)}(J))\ast k(j_{\mathds{L}(A)}(I))=k(j_{\mathds{L}(A)}(J))\ast k(j_{\mathds{L}(A)}(I))

\displaystyle=k(j_{\mathds{L}(A)}(J\ast I))=k(j_{\mathds{L}(A)}(I\ast J))=\varphi(I\ast J)=(\varphi\circ m)(I\otimes J)

where we have used the commutativity of the ideal multiplication in
\mathds{I}(A)
.

Since closed two-sided ideals are always self-adjoint (cf.
[
12
, Cor. 4.2.10]
), the involution on
\mathds{I}(A)
is always the identity. Hence, if
A
is
commutative
, we conclude that
\Omega_{q}(A)
is isomorphic to the frame
(\mathds{I}(A),m)
provided with the identity as involution. Thus, in the commutative setting the
spectrum of
A
is equivalent to the
traditional spectrum of
A
given by the frame of all closed ideals of
A
.

Since the spectrum of a unital
C^{*}
-algebra
A
is balanced and bisymmetric, the construction in Example
2.13
has a categorical base. Indeed,
\mathds{L}(A)\otimes\mathds{R}(A)
is the coproduct of
\mathds{L}(A)
and
\mathds{R}(A)
with the coprojections
j_{\mathds{L}(A)}
and
j_{\mathds{R}(A)}
in
\mathsf{BSQuant}
(cf.
[

, Thm. 2.5.9]
). Hence, the coequalizer

{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{3.0pt}{-1.68301pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 4.15 -2.33)} \pgfsys@hbox{58}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{}{{

{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-1.44441pt}{1.68301pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 -2 2.33)} \pgfsys@hbox{58}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\mathds{M}axA}
such that
\widehat{h}\circ\pi=h
holds.
In particular,
\widehat{h}\circ\pi\circ j_{\mathds{L}(A)}=h\circ j_{\mathds{L}(A)}=\iota_{\mathds{L}(A)}
follows, and so
\pi\circ j_{\mathds{L}(A)}
is injective — i.e. a monomorphism in
\mathsf{Quant}
. Further, the image of
\pi\circ j_{\mathds{L}(A)}
is contained in
\mathds{L}(\Omega_{q}(A))
. On the other hand, we choose
a\otimes b\in\mathds{L}(A)\otimes\mathds{R}(A)
and assume that
\pi(a\otimes b)
is left-sided in
\Omega_{q}(A)
. Then the relation

\pi(a\otimes b)=\pi(a\otimes(A\ast b))
follows. Since
A\ast b
is a closed two-sided ideal of
A
, the construction of
\pi
implies:

\displaystyle\pi(a\otimes b)

\displaystyle=\pi(a\otimes(A\ast b))=\pi((A\otimes(A\ast b))\star(a\otimes A))=\pi(A\otimes(A\ast b))\ast\pi(a\otimes A)

\displaystyle=\pi((A\ast b)\otimes A)\ast\pi(a\otimes A)=\pi(((A\ast b)\ast a)\otimes A)=(\pi\circ j_{\mathds{L}(A)})(A\ast(b\ast a)).

Now we use the fact that every tensor is a join of an appropriate family of elementary tensors and conclude from the previous relation that
\mathds{L}(\Omega_{q}(A))\subseteq(\pi\circ j_{\mathds{L}(A)})(\mathds{L}(A))
holds.
Hence,
\pi\circ j_{\mathds{L}(A)}
can be viewed as an isomorphism onto
\mathds{L}(\Omega_{q}(A))
in the sense of
\mathsf{Quant}
and
\mathsf{BSQuant}
, respectively.
In particular,
\mathds{L}(\Omega_{q})
satisfies condition
(A)
.

More precisely, this situation can be expressed by the following commutative diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathds{L}(A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathds{M}axA}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathds{R}(A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathds{L}(\Omega_{q}(A))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega_{q}(A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega_{q}(A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathds{I}(A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathds{L}(A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathds{L}(A)\otimes\mathds{R}(A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathds{R}(A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathds{I}(A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathds{I}(\Omega_{q}(A))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathds{L}(\Omega_{q}(A))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathds{L}(\Omega_{q}(A))\otimes\mathds{R}(\Omega_{q}(A))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathds{R}(\Omega_{q}(A))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathds{I}(\Omega_{q}(A))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{\mathds{L}(A)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{\mathds{R}(A)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{h}_{\mathds{L}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{\mathds{L}(\Omega_{q}(A))}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{h}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{\mathds{R}(\Omega_{q}(A))}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{h}_{\mathds{R}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{\mathds{L}(A)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j_{\mathds{L}(A)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{\mathds{L}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j_{\mathds{R}(A)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{\mathds{R}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{\mathds{R}(A)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{\mathds{L}(\Omega_{q}(A))}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{h}_{\mathds{I}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j_{\mathds{L}(\Omega_{q}(A))}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{h}_{\mathds{L}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{h}_{\mathds{L}}\otimes\widehat{h}_{\mathds{R}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j_{\mathds{R}(\Omega_{q}(A))}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{h}_{\mathds{R}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{\mathds{R}(\Omega_{q}(A))}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{h}_{\mathds{I}}}

where
\pi_{\mathds{L}}(\alpha)=\pi(\alpha\otimes\top)
for
\alpha\in\mathds{L}(\mathfrak{Q})
,
\widehat{h}_{\mathds{L}}
is the inverse map of
\pi_{\mathds{L}}
— i.e.
\widehat{h}_{\mathds{L}}(\varkappa)=\alpha
if and only if
\varkappa=\pi(\alpha\otimes\top)
for
\varkappa\in\mathds{L}(\Omega_{q}(A))
and
\alpha\in\mathds{L}(\mathfrak{Q})
,
\widehat{h}_{\mathds{R}}
is the inverse map of
\pi_{\mathds{R}}
— i.e.
\widehat{h}_{\mathds{R}}(\varkappa)=\beta
if and only if
\varkappa=\pi(\top\otimes\beta)
for
\varkappa\in\mathds{R}(\Omega_{q}(A))
and
\beta\in\mathds{R}(\mathfrak{Q})
, and
\widehat{h}_{\mathds{I}}(\delta)=\gamma
if and only if
\delta=\pi(\gamma\otimes\top)=\pi(\top\otimes\gamma)
for
\delta\in\mathds{I}(\Omega_{q}(A))
and
\gamma\in\mathds{I}(A)
. Obviously,
\widehat{h}_{\mathds{I}}
is a quantale isomorphism.

Now we conclude from the commutativity of the diagram that
\mathds{L}(A)\otimes\mathds{R}(A)\mathrel{\hbox to14.68pt{\vbox to13.05pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 4.33778pt\lower-5.01602pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.7pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{{}}{}

}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-35.76834pt}{-7.73766pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 -49.49 -10.71)} \pgfsys@hbox{58}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\mathds{L}(\Omega_{q}(A))\otimes\mathds{R}(\Omega_{q}(A))
. Hence the spectrum
\Omega_{q}(A)
satisfies condition
(C)
. Since condition
(B)
is obvious, we state the following important

Fact
.

The spectrum
\Omega_{q}(A)
of a unital
C^{*}
-algebra
A
is always a quantic frame. In this context, the commutative quantale
\mathds{I}(\Omega_{q}(A))
is considered as the
commutative part
of
\Omega_{q}(A)
.

3.
Quantale-enriched involutive topological spaces

To begin, we revisit the concept of quantale-enriched topological spaces (cf.
[
5
]
). Let
\mathfrak{Q}=(\mathfrak{Q},m,e)
be a unital quantale, and consider
\mathfrak{Q}
as a right (left)
\mathfrak{Q}
-module with respect to the multiplication
m
. Obviously, a right (left)
\mathfrak{Q}
-submodule of
\mathfrak{Q}
is a subquantale of
\mathfrak{Q}
.

Let
X
be a set. The free right
\mathfrak{Q}
-module on
X
is the complete lattice
\mathfrak{Q}^{X}
,
ordered pointwise and equipped with the right action
\boxdot
determined by the right quantale multiplication
— i.e.
(f\boxdot\alpha)(x)=f(x)\ast\alpha
for each
\alpha\in\mathfrak{Q}
,
x\in X
and
f\in\mathfrak{Q}^{X}
(cf.
[
11
]
). Since every set
X
can be viewed as a discrete
\mathfrak{Q}
-enriched category, a

\mathfrak{Q}
-valued map
f\in\mathfrak{Q}^{X}
is also referred to as a
\mathfrak{Q}
-presheaf on
X
.

A complete sublattice
\mathcal{T}\subseteq\mathfrak{Q}^{X}
is a
\mathfrak{Q}
-submodule of
\mathfrak{Q}^{X}
if the inclusion map is a right
\mathfrak{Q}
-module homomorphism — i.e.
\mathcal{T}\xhookrightarrow{\,\,\,}\mathfrak{Q}^{X}
is join-preserving and
f\ast\alpha\in\mathcal{T}
for all
f\in\mathcal{T}
and
\alpha\in\mathfrak{Q}
. A constant
\mathfrak{Q}
-presheaf on
X
that takes the value
\alpha
will always be denoted by
\underline{\alpha}
.

Further, let
\diamond
be a quasi-magma on
\mathfrak{Q}

(cf.
[
5
, Def. 1]
), which is a binary operation in the sense of the category of preordered sets satisfying:

\alpha\ast(\beta\diamond\gamma)\leq(\alpha\ast\beta)\diamond\gamma\quad\text{and}\quad(\beta\diamond\gamma)\ast\alpha\leq\beta\diamond(\gamma\ast\alpha),\quad\alpha,\beta,\gamma\in\mathfrak{Q}.

Obviously, the quantale multiplication is always a quasi-magma on
\mathfrak{Q}
.

A
\mathfrak{Q}
-submodule
\mathcal{T}
of
\mathfrak{Q}^{X}
in the sense of
\mathsf{Sup}
is a
\mathfrak{Q}
-enriched topology
on
X
if
\mathcal{T}
satisfies:

(T1)

\underline{\top}
is an element of
\mathcal{T}
.

(T2)

If
f_{1},f_{2}\in\mathcal{T}
then
f_{1}\diamond f_{2}\in\mathcal{T}
, where
f_{1}\diamond f_{2}
is defined pointwise.

Elements of a
\mathfrak{Q}
-enriched topology
\mathcal{T}
on
X
are called
open
\mathfrak{Q}
-presheaves
. The trivial
\mathfrak{Q}
-enriched topology on
X
coincides with the
\mathfrak{Q}
-submodule of all constant
\mathfrak{Q}
-presheaves attaining all left-sided elements of
\mathfrak{Q}
.

A
\mathfrak{Q}
-enriched topological space
(
\mathfrak{Q}
-topological space for short) is a pair
(X,\mathcal{T})
such that
X
is a set and
\mathcal{T}
is a
\mathfrak{Q}
-enriched topology on
X
. A
\mathfrak{Q}
-topological space
(Z,\mathcal{T}_{Z})
is a
\mathfrak{Q}
-topological subspace
of
(X,\mathcal{T})
, if there exists an injective map
Z\mathrel{\hbox to15.26pt{\vbox to14.41pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 4.6295pt\lower-5.01602pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.7pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}{}{}{{{}{}}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{}{{{}{}}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 7.33 0}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{5.65002pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 7.82 0)} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray=none,stroke-dashoffset=0.0pt} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap=round} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin=round} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -3.38 4.07 C -2.76 1.63 -1.38 0.47 0 0 C -1.38 -0.47 -2.76 -1.63 -3.38 -4.07}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{3.0pt}{-1.68301pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 4.15 -2.33)} \pgfsys@hbox{58}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-1.2965pt}{3.0441pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 -1.79 4.21)} \pgfsys@hbox{58}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}X
such that
\mathcal{T}_{Z}=\{\,f\circ\varphi\mid f\in\mathcal{T}\,\}

— i.e.
\mathcal{T}_{Z}
is the initial structure on
Z
with respect to
\varphi
and
(X,\mathcal{T})
.
Sometimes
\mathcal{T}_{Z}
is referred to as the
relative
\mathfrak{Q}
-enriched topology
on
Z
induced by
\mathcal{T}
and
\varphi
.
In many instances,
Z
is a subset of
X
and
\varphi
is the inclusion map
Z\xhookrightarrow{\,\,\,}X
.

Next, we recall the lower separation axioms for
\mathfrak{Q}
-topological spaces. A
\mathfrak{Q}
-topological space is classified as follows (for more details we refer to
[
1
,
5
]
):

–

Kolmogoroff separated
(
\mbox{\rm T}_{0}
-space) For each
x,y\in X
with
x\neq y
there exists
f\in\mathcal{T}
such that
f(x)\neq f(y)

–

Fréchet separated
(
\mbox{\rm T}_{1}
-space) For each
x,y\in X
with
x\neq y
there exist
f_{1},f_{2}\in\mathcal{T}
such that
f_{1}(x)\not\leq f_{1}(y)
and
f_{2}(y)\not\leq f_{2}(x)
.

–

Hausdorff separated
(
\mbox{\rm T}_{2}
-space) For each
x,y\in X
with
x\neq y
there exist
f_{1},f_{2}\in\mathcal{T}
such that

f_{1}(x)\diamond f_{2}(y)\not\leq\mathop{\textstyle\bigvee}\limits_{z\in X}f_{1}(z)\diamond f_{2}(z)\quad\text{or}\quad f_{2}(y)\diamond f_{1}(x)\not\leq\mathop{\textstyle\bigvee}\limits_{z\in X}f_{2}(z)\diamond f_{1}(z).

After these general remarks on
\mathfrak{Q}
-topological spaces, we now additionally consider
\mathfrak{Q}=(\mathfrak{Q},m,e,\ell)
as an involutive quantale. It is natural to incorporate the involution
\ell
into the axioms of
\mathfrak{Q}
-enriched topologies. To do so, we require that the quasi-magma
\diamond
on
\mathfrak{Q}
is
involutive
, meaning:

(\alpha\diamond\beta)^{\prime}=\beta^{\prime}\diamond\alpha^{\prime},\qquad\alpha,\beta\in\mathfrak{Q}.

A
\mathfrak{Q}
-enriched topology
\mathcal{T}
on
X
is
involutive
if it satisfies the following additional condition:

(T3)

If
f\in\mathcal{T}
then
f^{\prime}\in\mathcal{T}
.

Consequently,
(X,\mathcal{T})
is called an involutive
\mathfrak{Q}
-enriched topological space
.

The axiom
(T3)
implies that involutive
\mathfrak{Q}
-enriched topologies on
X
are
\mathfrak{Q}
-sub-bimodules
of the
\mathfrak{Q}
-bimodule
\mathfrak{Q}^{X}
, where the left action is determined by the left quantale multiplication. In this context, it is important to note that the subquantale
\mathds{L}(\mathcal{T})
of all
left-sided open

\mathfrak{Q}
-presheaves of an involutive
\mathfrak{Q}
-enriched topology
\mathcal{T}
is always a
\mathfrak{Q}
-enriched topology, but not involutive.

Let
\mathfrak{S}
be an involutive subquantale of
\mathfrak{Q}
, and suppose
\mathfrak{S}
is also a right
\mathfrak{Q}
-submodule of
\mathfrak{Q}
. An involutive
\mathfrak{Q}
-enriched topological space
(X,\mathcal{T})
is called an
involutive
\mathfrak{S}
-sober space
if
(X,\mathcal{T})
is a
\mbox{{T}}_{0}
-space and every strong, involutive quantale homomorphism
\mathcal{T}\mathrel{\hbox to17.21pt{\vbox to14.82pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 5.6045pt\lower-5.01602pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.7pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}{}{}{{{}{}}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{}{{{}{}}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 7.33 0}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{5.65002pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 7.82 0)} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray=none,stroke-dashoffset=0.0pt} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap=round} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin=round} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -3.38 4.07 C -2.76 1.63 -1.38 0.47 0 0 C -1.38 -0.47 -2.76 -1.63 -3.38 -4.07}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{3.0pt}{-1.68301pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 4.15 -2.33)} \pgfsys@hbox{58}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-2.2715pt}{1.68301pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 -3.14 2.33)} \pgfsys@hbox{58}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\mathfrak{S}
, which is also a right
\mathfrak{Q}
-module homomorphism, is induced by an element
p_{0}\in X
— this means that
K(f)=f(p_{0})
holds for all
f\in\mathcal{T}
.

Let
\mathcal{T}
be a
\mathfrak{Q}
-topology on a set
X
. A subset
\mathcal{B}\subseteq\mathcal{T}
is called a
base
of
\mathcal{T}
if every open
\mathfrak{Q}
-presheaf in
\mathcal{T}
can be expressed as a join of elements from
\mathcal{B}
.
A subset
\mathcal{S}
of
\mathfrak{Q}^{X}
is called a
subbase
of an involutive
\mathfrak{Q}
-enriched topology
\mathcal{T}
on
X
if

\mathcal{T}=\mathop{\textstyle\bigcap}\{\,\mathcal{W}\mid\mathcal{S}\subseteq\mathcal{W}\text{ and }\mathcal{W}\text{ is an involutive $\mathfrak{Q}$-\hskip 0.0ptenriched topology on $X$}\,\}.

Obviously, a subbase
\mathcal{S}
of an involutive
\mathfrak{Q}
-enriched topology
\mathcal{T}
is not necessarily a subbase of
\mathcal{T}
in the sense of general
\mathfrak{Q}
-enriched topologies.
There exist simple counterexamples in this respect.

A characterization of
\mathfrak{Q}
-interior operators (cf.
[
5
]
) in the context of involutive
\mathfrak{Q}
-enriched topologies can be given as follows.

Lemma 3.1
.

Let
\mathcal{T}
be a
\mathfrak{Q}
-enriched topology on
X
and
\mathcal{I}

be the corresponding
\mathfrak{Q}
-interior operator. Then
\mathcal{T}
is involutive if and only if
\mathcal{I}
satisfies the following condition
:

(I3)

\mathcal{I}(f)^{\prime}\leq\mathcal{I}(f^{\prime}),\qquad f\in\mathfrak{Q}^{X}
.

Proof.

The sufficiency is immediate. Conversely, if
\mathcal{T}
is an involutive
\mathfrak{Q}
-enriched topology on
X
, then
I(f)^{\prime}\in\mathcal{T}
for all
f\in\mathfrak{Q}
and thus
I(f)^{\prime}\leq I(I(f)^{\prime})\leq I(f^{\prime})
.
∎

The previous condition is obviously equivalent to

(I3
′
)

\mathcal{I}(f)^{\prime}=\mathcal{I}(f^{\prime}),\qquad f\in\mathfrak{Q}^{X}
.

Consequently, if the quasi-magma
\diamond
coincides with the quantale multiplication, then Lemma
3.1
states that the
\mathfrak{Q}
-interior operator of any involutive
\mathfrak{Q}
-enriched topology on
X
is always an involutive conucleus on
\mathfrak{Q}^{X}
with respect to the pointwise defined quantale multiplication.

4.
The quantization of
\boldsymbol{2}
and quantized topological spaces

Quantized topological spaces naturally emerge as a distinct subclass of quantale-enriched topological spaces through the topologization of quantales. This construction is fundamentally based on the quantization of
\boldsymbol{2}
.

Since the concept of open
\mathfrak{Q}
-presheaves requires a
unital
quantale
\mathfrak{Q}
and the quantization
\mathfrak{Q}_{\boldsymbol{2}}
of
\boldsymbol{2}
is non-unital, we are interested in unital and non-integral quantales
\mathfrak{Q}
such that
\mathfrak{Q}_{\boldsymbol{2}}
can be embedded as a maximal strong subquantale of
\mathfrak{Q}
. This approach leads to the concept of strictly quantized, unital quantales.

A unital quantale
\widetilde{\mathfrak{Q}_{2}}
is
quantized
, if
\mathfrak{Q}_{\boldsymbol{2}}
is a subquantale of
\widetilde{\mathfrak{Q}_{2}}
and the inclusion map is a strong quantale homomorphism. It is
strictly quantized
if, additionally, the unital subquantale generated by
\mathfrak{Q}_{\boldsymbol{2}}
coincides with
\widetilde{\mathfrak{Q}_{2}}
(cf.
[
8
, Def. 2.1 and 2.4]
).
Thus, a strictly quantized, unital quantale contains at most
11
elements. Besides the unit
e
and the elements of
\mathfrak{Q}_{\boldsymbol{2}}
, the remaining elements of
\widetilde{\mathfrak{Q}_{2}}
are denoted by
\widetilde{b}:=b\vee e
,
\widetilde{a}_{\ell}:=a_{\ell}\vee e
,
\widetilde{a}_{r}:=a_{r}\vee e
and
\widetilde{c}:=c\vee e
. Obviously
\widetilde{\mathfrak{Q}_{2}}
is always pre-idempotent, and the cardinality of
\widetilde{\mathfrak{Q}_{2}}
depends on the order-theoretical position of the unit (cf.
[
8
]
). Up to an isomorphism there exist
6
different strictly quantized, unital quantales (cf.
[
8
, Figures 2, 4 and 5]
).

Since the unit in a quantale is always hermitian, the involution on
\mathfrak{Q}_{\boldsymbol{2}}
extends uniquely to an involution on
\widetilde{\mathfrak{Q}_{2}}
. Therefore, the embedding
\mathfrak{Q}_{\boldsymbol{2}}\xhookrightarrow{\,\,\,}\widetilde{\mathfrak{Q}_{2}}
is always involutive, and
\mathfrak{Q}_{\boldsymbol{2}}
becomes an involutive subquantale of
\widetilde{\mathfrak{Q}_{2}}
.

Furthermore, recall that every unital quantale can be regarded as a right
\mathfrak{Q}
-module via right quantale multiplication. In this context,
\mathfrak{Q}_{\boldsymbol{2}}
is a right
\widetilde{\mathfrak{Q}_{2}}
-submodule of
\widetilde{\mathfrak{Q}_{2}}
. Since the elements
\widetilde{a_{\ell}}
and
\widetilde{a_{r}}
are always present in any strictly quantized unital quantale and the relations

\alpha\ast\widetilde{b}=\alpha\ast\widetilde{a_{\ell}}=\alpha\quad\text{and}\quad\alpha\ast a_{r}=\alpha\ast\top

hold for all
\alpha\in\mathfrak{Q}_{\boldsymbol{2}}
, the property of a subset of
\mathfrak{Q}_{\boldsymbol{2}}
being a
\widetilde{\mathfrak{Q}_{2}}
-submodule of
\mathfrak{Q}_{2}
does
not
depend on the specific choice of strictly quantized, unital quantale. In fact, there exist three
\widetilde{\mathfrak{Q}_{2}}
-submodules of
\mathfrak{Q}_{\boldsymbol{2}}
that contain the universal upper bound
\top
of
\mathfrak{Q}_{\boldsymbol{2}}
:

\{\bot,a_{\ell},\top\},\quad\{\bot,a_{\ell},c,\top\}\quad\text{and}\quad\mathfrak{Q}_{\boldsymbol{2}}.

All of these
\widetilde{\mathfrak{Q}_{2}}
-submodules are necessarily subquantales of
\mathfrak{Q}_{\boldsymbol{2}}
, but only
\mathfrak{Q}_{\boldsymbol{2}}
itself is involutive.

Since we are not concerned with the cardinality of strictly quantized unital quantales, we will choose a strictly quantized unital quantale
\widetilde{\mathfrak{Q}_{2}}
and establish the following:

Standing Assumption
.

In what follows,
\widetilde{\mathfrak{Q}_{2}}
will always denote a fixed strictly quantized unital quantale, and the quasi-magma
\diamond
will always coincide with the quantale multiplication
\ast
.

A
quantized topological space
is a
\widetilde{\mathfrak{Q}_{2}}
-topological space
(X,\mathcal{T})
satisfying the additional property that the range of every open
\widetilde{\mathfrak{Q}_{2}}
-presheaf is contained in
\mathfrak{Q}_{2}
— i.e.
\mathcal{T}
is a
\widetilde{\mathfrak{Q}_{2}}
-submodule of
\mathfrak{Q}_{\boldsymbol{2}}^{X}
(cf.
[
8
, Section 5]
).
Since both the right action on
\mathcal{T}
and the formation of joins in
\mathcal{T}
are defined pointwise, it follows from the previous observations that the property of being a right
\widetilde{\mathfrak{Q}_{\boldsymbol{2}}}
-submodule of
\mathfrak{Q}_{\boldsymbol{2}}^{X}
is independent of the specific choice of strictly quantized, unital quantales. For this reason,
\mathcal{T}
is also referred to a
quantized topology
. A quantized topological space
(X,\mathcal{T})
is
involutive
if
\mathcal{T}
satisfies the axiom (
T
3).

A quantized topological space
(X,\mathcal{T})
is
strongly Hausdorff separated
if for each
x,y\in X
with
x\neq y
there exists a left-sided
f_{1}\in\mathcal{T}
and a right-sided
f_{2}\in\mathcal{T}
such that

f_{2}(x)\star f_{1}(y)\not\leq\mathop{\textstyle\bigvee}\limits_{z\in X}(f_{2}(z)\star f_{1}(z)).

We show that the strong Hausdorff separation has a
convergent-theoretical
characterization.
For this purpose we recall that an isotone map
\mathfrak{Q}^{X}\mathrel{\hbox to15.04pt{\vbox to13.05pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 4.51851pt\lower-5.01602pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.7pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}{}{}{{{}{}}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{}{{{}{}}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 7.33 0}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{5.65002pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 7.82 0)} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray=none,stroke-dashoffset=0.0pt} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap=round} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin=round} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -3.38 4.07 C -2.76 1.63 -1.38 0.47 0 0 C -1.38 -0.47 -2.76 -1.63 -3.38 -4.07}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{3.0pt}{-1.68301pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 4.15 -2.33)} \pgfsys@hbox{58}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-1.1855pt}{1.68301pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 -1.64 2.33)} \pgfsys@hbox{58}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\mathfrak{Q}
is called a
\mathfrak{Q}
-enriched filter (
\mathfrak{Q}
-filter for short) (cf.
[
5
, Def. 3]
), if
\omega
satisfies the following conditions:

(F0)

\omega(f)\ast\alpha\leq\omega(f\ast\alpha),\qquad\alpha\in\mathfrak{Q},\,f\in\mathfrak{Q}^{X}
,

(F1)

\omega(\underline{\top})=\top
,

(F2)

\omega(f_{1})\ast\omega(f_{2})\leq\omega(f_{1}\ast f_{2}),\qquad f_{1},f_{2}\in\mathfrak{Q}^{X}
,

(F3)

\omega(f)\leq\mathop{\textstyle\bigvee}_{x\in X}f(x),\qquad f\in\mathfrak{Q}^{X}
.

An element
x\in X
is a
left
(resp.
right
)
limit
of
\omega
if
\nu_{x}(f)\leq\omega(f)
for every left-sided (resp. right-sided)
f\in\mathfrak{Q}^{X}
, where
(\nu_{x})_{x\in X}
denotes the
\mathfrak{Q}
-neighborhood system corresponding to
(X,\mathcal{T})
(cf.
[
5
]
).

Theorem 4.1
.

Let
(X,\mathcal{T})
be a quantized topological space.
Then the following are equivalent
:

(i)

(X,\mathcal{T})
is strongly Hausdorff separated.

(ii)

For each
\widetilde{\mathfrak{Q}_{2}}
-filter
\omega
on
X
and each
x,y\in X
such that
x
is left limit and
y
is right limit of
\omega
the relation
x=y
holds — i.e. if
\omega
has left a limit and a right limit, then they coincide.

Proof.

(i)
\implies
(ii): Let us assume that
x
is a left limit of
\omega

and
y
is a right limit of
\omega
. Then for every left-sided open
\widetilde{\mathfrak{Q}_{2}}
-presheaf
f_{1}
and for every right-sided open
\widetilde{\mathfrak{Q}_{2}}
-presheaf
f_{2}
we obtain:

f_{2}(y)\star f_{1}(x)=\nu_{y}(f_{2})\star\nu_{x}(f_{1})\leq\omega(f_{2})\star\omega(f_{1})\leq\omega(f_{2}\star f_{1})\leq\mathop{\textstyle\bigvee}\limits_{z\in X}(f_{2}(z)\star f_{1}(z)).

Hence the strong Hausdorff separation axiom implies
x=y
.

(ii)
\implies
(i): Assume that
(X,\mathcal{T})
is not strongly Hausdorff separated. Then there exists
x,y\in X
with
x\neq y
such that for all left-sided open
\widetilde{\mathfrak{Q}_{2}}
-presheaves
f_{1}\in\mathcal{T}
and for all right-sided open
\widetilde{\mathfrak{Q}_{2}}
-presheaves
f_{2}\in\mathcal{T}
, the following relation holds:

(4.1)

f_{2}(y)\star f_{1}(x)\leq\mathop{\textstyle\bigvee}\limits_{z\in X}(f_{2}(z)\star f_{1}(z)).

Now we construct a
\widetilde{\mathfrak{Q}_{2}}
-filter as follows:

\omega(h)=\mathop{\textstyle\bigvee}\{\,g(y)\star f(x)\mid f,g\in\mathcal{T}\text{ with }\top\star f\leq f,\,\,g\star\top\leq g\text{ and }g\star f\leq h\,\},\qquad h\in\widetilde{\mathfrak{Q}_{2}}^{X}.

Obviously,
\omega
is isotone and satisfies axiom (F1). If
f
is a left-sided open
\widetilde{\mathfrak{Q}_{2}}
-presheaf, then
f\star\alpha
is also a left-sided open
\widetilde{\mathfrak{Q}_{2}}
-presheaf
for all
\alpha\in\mathfrak{Q}
. Hence
\omega
satisfies (F0).

To verify (F2), we proceed as follows. Let
h_{1},h_{2}\in\widetilde{\mathfrak{Q}_{2}}^{X}
and
f_{1},g_{1},f_{2},g_{2}\in\mathcal{T}
such that
\top\star f_{1}\leq f_{1}
,
g_{1}\star\top\leq g_{1}
,
g_{1}\star f_{1}\leq h_{1}
,
\top\star f_{2}\leq f_{2}
,
g_{2}\star\top\leq g_{2}
and
g_{2}\star f_{2}\leq h_{2}
. Since
(X,\mathcal{T})
is
quantized
and
\mathfrak{Q}_{\boldsymbol{2}}
is
bisymmetric
, we have

f_{1}\star f_{2},g_{1}\star g_{2}\in\mathcal{T}
,
\top\star(f_{1}\star f_{2})\leq f_{1}\star f_{2}
,
(g_{1}\star g_{2})\star\top\leq g_{1}\star g_{2}
,
(g_{1}\star g_{2})\star(f_{1}\star f_{2})=(g_{1}\star f_{1})\star(g_{2}\star f_{2})\leq h_{1}\star h_{2}
and

(g_{1}(y)\star f_{1}(x))\star(g_{2}(y)\star f_{2}(x))=(g_{1}\star g_{2})(y)\star(f_{1}\star f_{2})(x)\leq\omega(h_{1}\star h_{2}).

Hence,
\omega(h_{1})\star\omega(h_{2})\leq\omega(h_{1}\star h_{2})
follows. Finally, (F3) is a consequence of (
4.1
).

We show now that
x
is left limit point of
\omega
. Let
h\in\widetilde{\mathfrak{Q}_{2}}^{X}
be a left-sided
\widetilde{\mathfrak{Q}_{2}}
-presheaf and
f\in\mathcal{T}
such that
f\leq h
. Then
\top\star f
is a left-sided open
\widetilde{\mathfrak{Q}_{2}}
-presheaf,
\underline{\top}
a right-sided open
\widetilde{\mathfrak{Q}_{2}}
-presheaf and
\underline{\top}\star(\top\star f)\leq\top\star h\leq h
. Hence,
\top\star f(x)=\underline{\top}(y)\star(\top\star f)(x)\leq\omega(h)
. Since
(X,\mathcal{T})
is quantized and
\mathfrak{Q}_{\boldsymbol{2}}
is
semi-unital
, we conclude that
f(x)\leq\omega(h)
, and so

\displaystyle\nu_{x}(h)

\displaystyle=\mathop{\textstyle\bigvee}\{\,f(x)\mid f\in\mathcal{T}\text{ and }f\leq h\,\}\leq\omega(h).

Analogously, we show that
y
is a right limit of
\omega
. Since
x\neq y
, we arrive at a contradiction of (ii). Hence, the assumption is false, and (i) holds.
∎

From the perspective of convergence theory the previous theorem underlines the importance of the strong Hausdorff separation axiom for quantized topological spaces.

4.1.
The topological characterization of strongly spatial quantales

defined by:

(4.2)

\mathds{A}_{\alpha}(p)=h_{p}(\alpha),\qquad p\in\sigma_{s}(\mathfrak{Q}).

\widetilde{\mathfrak{Q}_{2}}
-enriched topology generated by the subbase

\mathcal{S}=\{\,\mathds{A}_{\alpha}\mid\alpha\in{\mathfrak{Q}}\,\}
.

\mathds{A}_{\alpha}\star a_{\ell}=\mathds{A}_{\alpha\ast\top}\star a_{\ell}
for each

\alpha\in\mathfrak{Q}
, we conclude from (
2.7
) and (
2.8
) that the quantized topology on
\sigma_{s(\mathfrak{Q})}
generated by
\mathcal{S}
is given by

\mathcal{T}_{\sigma_{s}(\mathfrak{Q})}=\{\,\mathds{A}_{\alpha}\vee(\mathds{A}_{\beta}\star a_{\ell})\mid\alpha\in\mathfrak{Q},\,\beta\in\mathds{R}(\mathfrak{Q})\,\}.

Further, we consider the tensor product
(\mathfrak{Q}\otimes C_{3},m\otimes m_{\ell})
of the quantales
(\mathfrak{Q},m)
and
C_{3}^{\ell}
. Then

\mathcal{U}_{\mathfrak{Q}}=\{\,(\alpha\otimes\top)\vee(\beta\otimes a)\mid\alpha\in\mathfrak{Q},\,\beta\in\mathds{R}(\mathfrak{Q}),\,\alpha\leq\beta\,\}

is a subquantale of
(\mathfrak{Q}\otimes C_{3},m\otimes m_{\ell})
. In fact, if
(\alpha_{1}\otimes\top)\vee(\beta_{1}\otimes a),(\alpha_{2}\otimes\top)\vee(\beta_{2}\otimes a)\in\mathcal{U}_{\mathfrak{Q}}
then

((\alpha_{1}\otimes\top)\vee(\beta_{1}\otimes a))\star((\alpha_{2}\otimes\top)\vee(\beta_{2}\otimes a))=((\beta_{1}\ast\alpha_{2})\otimes\top)\vee((\beta_{1}\ast\beta_{2})\otimes a)
.

Proposition 4.2
.

, Prop. 5.5]
)
Let

.
Then

is strongly spatial if and only if the quantales

.

Necessity
:

). Hence, the map
\mathfrak{Q}\mathrel{\hbox to15.26pt{\vbox to14.41pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 4.6295pt\lower-5.01602pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.7pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{{}}{}

\lxSVG@closescope }}}\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{}{{

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\mathcal{U}_{\mathfrak{Q}}
defined by

\varphi(\alpha)=(\alpha\otimes\top)\vee((\alpha\ast\top)\otimes a),\qquad\alpha\in\mathfrak{Q},

is an injective and strong quantale homomorphism. The isomorphism between
\mathcal{T}_{\sigma_{s(\mathfrak{Q})}}
and
\mathcal{U}_{\mathfrak{Q}}
follows from
[
8
, Prop. 5.5 and Rem. 5.6]
.

Sufficiency

Since every injective and join-preserving map reflects the respective
orders, we conclude that
\mathfrak{Q}
is isomorphic to a subquantale of
\mathcal{T}_{\sigma_{s(\mathfrak{Q})}}
containing the universal upper bound of
\mathcal{T}_{\sigma_{s(\mathfrak{Q})}}
. Since every quantized topology is a strongly spatial quantale, the result follows.
∎

In the following, we assume that
\mathfrak{Q}
is always an
involutive
quantale.

and identify every hermitian strong prime element

determined by

Proposition 4.3
.

Let
\mathfrak{Q}
be an involutive quantale and

be its hermitian spectrum. Then the involutive quantized topological space

(

)
is an involutive
\mathfrak{Q}_{\boldsymbol{2}}
-sober space.

is again an involutive strong quantale homomorphism, and it is induced by the hermitian strong prime element
p_{0}=(K\circ\Phi)^{\vdash}(c)
— i.e.
K\circ\Phi=h_{p_{0}}
. In particular

(4.4)

K(\mathds{A}_{\alpha})=(K\circ\Phi)(\alpha)=h_{p_{0}}(\alpha)=\mathds{A}_{\alpha}(p_{0}),\qquad\alpha\in\mathfrak{Q}.

Since
K
is involutive and a right
\widetilde{\mathfrak{Q}_{2}}
-module
homomorphism, we conclude from (
4.4
) that for all
\beta\in\mathfrak{Q}
the following relations hold:

\displaystyle K(\mathds{A}_{\alpha}\star a_{\ell})=K(\mathds{A}_{\alpha})\star a_{\ell}=\mathds{A}_{\alpha}(p_{0})\star a_{\ell}=(\mathds{A}_{\alpha}\star a_{\ell})(p_{0}),

\displaystyle K(a_{r}\star\mathds{A}_{\alpha})=(K((\mathds{A}_{\alpha})^{\prime}\star a_{\ell}))^{\prime}=(K(\mathds{A}_{\alpha})^{\prime}\star a_{\ell})^{\prime}=(\mathds{A}_{\alpha}(p_{0})^{\prime}\star a_{\ell})^{\prime}=a_{r}\star\mathds{A}_{\alpha}(p_{0})=(a_{r}\star\mathds{A}_{\alpha})(p_{0}),

\displaystyle K(a_{r}\star\mathds{A}_{\alpha}\star a_{\ell})=K(a_{r}\star\mathds{A}_{\alpha})\star a_{\ell}=a_{r}\star\mathds{A}_{\alpha}(p_{0})\star a_{\ell}=(a_{r}\star\mathds{A}_{\alpha}\star a_{\ell})(p_{0}).

Hence,
K(g)=g(p_{0})
follows for all
g\in\mathcal{T}_{X}
(cf. (
4.3
)).
∎

In the following we are interested in involutive quantales such that the associated quantized topological spaces have Fréchet separated or strongly Hausdorff separated subspaces. As we will see, quantic frames will form an interesting class for this purpose.

given by

for each

on

\mathcal{T}_{Y}=\{\,f\circ\varphi\mid f\in\mathcal{T}_{X}\,\}.

Since in various cases the quantale

.

The aim of the following considerations is to construct a subbase of
\mathcal{T}_{Y}
.

induces an involutive strong quantale homomorphism
\mathfrak{Q}\mathrel{\hbox to28.89pt{\vbox to17.48pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 11.4433pt\lower-5.01602pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.7pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}{}{}{{{}{}}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{}{{{}{}}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 7.33 0}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{5.65002pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 7.82 0)} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray=none,stroke-dashoffset=0.0pt} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap=round} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin=round} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -3.38 4.07 C -2.76 1.63 -1.38 0.47 0 0 C -1.38 -0.47 -2.76 -1.63 -3.38 -4.07}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{}{{

,
and a pair of strong quantale homomorphisms
\mathds{L}(\mathfrak{Q})\mathrel{\hbox to18.88pt{\vbox to16.87pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 6.43977pt\lower-5.01602pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.7pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}{}{}{{{}{}}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{}{{{}{}}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 7.33 0}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{5.65002pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 7.82 0)} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray=none,stroke-dashoffset=0.0pt} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap=round} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin=round} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -3.38 4.07 C -2.76 1.63 -1.38 0.47 0 0 C -1.38 -0.47 -2.76 -1.63 -3.38 -4.07}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{}{{

and
\mathds{R}(\mathfrak{Q})\mathrel{\hbox to21.58pt{\vbox to17.68pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 7.79222pt\lower-5.01602pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.7pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{{}}{}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{3.0pt}{-1.68301pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 4.15 -2.33)} \pgfsys@hbox{58}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-4.45921pt}{4.46634pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 -6.17 6.18)} \pgfsys@hbox{58}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\mathfrak{Q}_{\boldsymbol{2}}
by (cf. (
2.9
)):

h_{p}(\alpha)=\begin{cases}\bot,&\alpha\ast\top\leq p,\\
a_{\ell},&\alpha\ast\top\not\leq p,\,\alpha\leq p,\\
\top,&\alpha\not\leq p,\end{cases}\quad h_{p^{\prime}}(\beta)=\begin{cases}\bot,&\top\ast\beta\leq p^{\prime},\\
a_{r},&\top\ast\beta\not\leq p^{\prime},\,\beta\leq p^{\prime},\\
\top,&\beta\not\leq p^{\prime},\end{cases}\quad\alpha\in\mathds{L}(\mathfrak{Q}),\,\beta\in\mathds{R}(\mathfrak{Q}).

Now we refer to Remark
2.9
, Proposition
2.10
(c) and (d), and Corollary
2.11
and observe that
\varphi(p)=p\vee p^{\prime}=\pi((p\otimes\top)\vee(\top\otimes p^{\prime}))\in\sigma_{h}(\mathfrak{Q})
and
\pi^{\vdash}(\varphi(p))=(p\otimes\top)\vee(\top\otimes p^{\prime})=r\in\sigma_{h}(\mathds{L}(\mathfrak{Q})\otimes\mathds{R}(\mathfrak{Q}))
.
Hence, we consider the composition
H_{r}=h_{\varphi(p)}\circ\pi
and conclude that it is the coproduct of
h_{p}
and
h_{p^{\prime}}
— i.e.
H_{r}=h_{p}\sqcup h_{p^{\prime}}
. Consequently, since
\mathfrak{Q}
is a quantic frame, the following diagram is commutative:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{Q}_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{Q}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathds{L}(\mathfrak{Q})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathds{L}(\mathfrak{Q})\otimes\mathds{R}(\mathfrak{Q})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathds{R}(\mathfrak{Q})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{\varphi(p)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j_{\mathds{L}(\mathfrak{Q})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{\mathds{L}(\mathfrak{Q})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{p}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{\mathds{R}(\mathfrak{Q})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j_{\mathds{R}(\mathfrak{Q})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{p^{\prime}}}

and for each
\alpha\in\mathds{L}(\mathfrak{Q})
and
\beta\in\mathds{R}(\mathfrak{Q})
the relation

h_{\varphi(p)}(\pi(\alpha\otimes\beta))=h_{\varphi(p)}(\pi(\top\otimes\beta))\star h_{\varphi(p)}(\pi(\alpha\otimes\top))=h_{p^{\prime}}(\beta)\star h_{p}(\alpha)

holds.
Since
\mathds{L}(\mathfrak{Q})\otimes\mathds{R}(\mathfrak{Q})\mathrel{\hbox to14.68pt{\vbox to13.05pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 4.33778pt\lower-5.01602pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.7pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}{}{}{{{}{}}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{}{{{}{}}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 7.33 0}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{5.65002pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 7.82 0)} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray=none,stroke-dashoffset=0.0pt} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap=round} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin=round} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -3.38 4.07 C -2.76 1.63 -1.38 0.47 0 0 C -1.38 -0.47 -2.76 -1.63 -3.38 -4.07}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{3.0pt}{-1.68301pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 4.15 -2.33)} \pgfsys@hbox{58}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-1.00478pt}{1.68301pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 -1.39 2.33)} \pgfsys@hbox{58}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\mathfrak{Q}
(cf. Remark
2.9
) is surjective and every tensor in
\mathds{L}(\mathfrak{Q})\otimes\mathds{R}(\mathfrak{Q})
is a join of elementary tensors,
we choose
\alpha\in\mathds{L}(\mathfrak{Q})
,
\beta\in\mathds{R}(\mathfrak{Q})
and
\mathds{A}_{\pi(\alpha\otimes\beta)}
as on open
\mathfrak{Q}
-presheaf on
X
. Then we refer to (
4.2
) and define:

(5.1)

\mathds{B}_{\alpha\otimes\beta}(p):=\mathds{A}_{\pi(\alpha\otimes\beta)}(\varphi(p))=h_{\varphi(p)}(\pi(\alpha\otimes\beta))=h_{p^{\prime}}(\beta)\star h_{p}(\alpha),\qquad p\in\sigma(\mathds{L}(\mathfrak{Q})).

Remark 5.1
.

Referring to the formulations of
h_{p}(\alpha)
and

h_{p^{\prime}}(\beta)
, it is not difficult to check that the following holds:

\mathds{B}_{\alpha\otimes\beta}(p)=\begin{cases}\bot,&\beta^{\prime}\ast\top\leq p\quad\text{or}\quad\alpha\ast\top\leq p,\\
b,&\beta^{\prime}\ast\top\not\leq p,\,\alpha\ast\top\not\leq p,\,\alpha\ast\beta^{\prime}\leq p,\,\beta^{\prime}\ast\alpha\leq p,\\
a_{\ell},&\beta^{\prime}\ast\top\not\leq p,\,\alpha\ast\top\not\leq p,\,\alpha\ast\beta^{\prime}\not\leq p,\,\beta^{\prime}\ast\alpha\leq p,\\
a_{r},&\beta^{\prime}\ast\top\not\leq p,\,\alpha\ast\top\not\leq p,\,\beta^{\prime}\ast\alpha\not\leq p,\,\alpha\ast\beta^{\prime}\leq p,\\
\top,&\alpha\ast\beta^{\prime}\not\leq p,\,\beta^{\prime}\ast\alpha\not\leq p,\end{cases}

where we have used frequently the primality of
p
.

After these simplifications we note that the involutive quantized topology
\mathcal{T}_{Y}
on
Y=\sigma(\mathds{L}(\mathfrak{Q}))
has the following base (cf. (
4.3
)):

(5.2)

\mathcal{B}=\{\,\mathds{B}_{\alpha_{1}\otimes\beta_{1}}\vee(\mathds{B}_{\alpha_{2}\otimes\beta_{2}}\star a_{\ell})\vee(a_{r}\star\mathds{B}_{\alpha_{3}\otimes\beta_{3}})\vee(a_{r}\star\mathds{B}_{\alpha_{4}\otimes\beta_{4}}\star a_{\ell})\mid\alpha_{i}\otimes\beta_{i}\in\mathds{L}(\mathfrak{Q})\otimes\mathds{R}(\mathfrak{Q}),\,i=1,..,4\,\}.

Proposition 5.2
.

Let
\mathfrak{Q}
be a quantic frame such that the subquantale
\mathds{L}(\mathfrak{Q})
is spatial, and let
(Y,\mathcal{T}_{Y})
be the associated involutive quantized topological space determined by
(
5.2
)
.

(a)

If
\mathds{B}_{\alpha_{1}\otimes\beta_{1}}
and
\mathds{B}_{\alpha_{2}\otimes\beta_{2}}
are open
\widetilde{\mathfrak{Q}_{2}}
-presheaves of
\mathcal{T}_{Y}
with
\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}\in\mathds{L}(\mathfrak{Q})
and
\beta_{1},\beta_{2}\in\mathds{R}(\mathfrak{Q})
, then

\mathds{B}_{\alpha_{1}\otimes\beta_{1}}=\mathds{B}_{\alpha_{2}\otimes\beta_{2}}
if and only if
\beta_{1}^{\prime}\ast\alpha_{1}=\beta_{2}^{\prime}\ast\alpha_{2}
and
\alpha_{1}\ast\beta_{1}^{\prime}=\alpha_{2}\ast\beta_{2}^{\prime}
.

(b)

For every left-sided open
\widetilde{\mathfrak{Q}_{2}}
-presheaf

f\in\mathcal{T}_{Y}
, there exists a unique pair
(\alpha,\gamma)\in{\mathds{L}(\mathfrak{Q})\times\mathds{I}(\mathfrak{Q})}
satisfying the following property
:

(5.3)

\alpha\leq\gamma\quad\text{and}\quad f=\mathds{B}_{\alpha\otimes\top}\vee\bigl(\mathds{B}_{\gamma\otimes\top}\star a_{\ell}\bigr).

(c)

If
\mathfrak{Q}
is a factor, then
\mathds{L}(\mathcal{T}_{Y})\setminus\{\underline{a_{\ell}}\}
is isomorphic to
\mathds{L}(\mathfrak{Q})
.

Proof.

(a) Let us assume
\mathds{B}_{\alpha_{1}\otimes\beta_{1}}=\mathds{B}_{\alpha_{2}\otimes\beta_{2}}
. Then Remark
5.1
implies that for each prime element
p
of
\mathds{L}({\mathfrak{Q}})
we have

\displaystyle\beta_{1}^{\prime}\ast\alpha_{1}\leq p

\displaystyle\iff\mathds{B}_{\alpha_{2}\otimes\beta_{2}}(p)=\mathds{B}_{\alpha_{1}\otimes\beta_{1}}(p)\leq a_{\ell}\iff\beta_{2}^{\prime}\ast\alpha_{2}\leq p

Since
\mathds{L}(\mathfrak{Q})
is spatial, it follows that
\beta_{1}^{\prime}\ast\alpha_{1}=\beta_{2}^{\prime}\ast\alpha_{2}
. The equality
\alpha_{1}\ast\beta_{1}^{\prime}=\alpha_{2}\ast\beta_{2}^{\prime}
can be proved in a similar way.
Conversely, if
\beta_{1}^{\prime}\ast\alpha_{1}=\beta_{2}^{\prime}\ast\alpha_{2}
and
\alpha_{1}\ast\beta_{1}^{\prime}=\alpha_{2}\ast\beta_{2}^{\prime}
, then Remark
5.1
implies that
\mathds{B}_{\alpha_{1}\otimes\beta_{1}}(p)=\mathds{B}_{\alpha_{2}\otimes\beta_{2}}(p)
for each
p\in\sigma(\mathds{L}(\mathfrak{Q})
.

(b) Since every two-sided element of
\mathfrak{Q}
is hermitian (cf.
(B)
), and in particular so is the universal upper bound in
\mathfrak{Q}_{\boldsymbol{2}}
, an application of (
5.1
) yields the following relation for all
p\in\sigma(\mathds{L}(\mathfrak{Q}))
:

\displaystyle\top\star\mathds{B}_{\alpha\otimes\beta}(p)

\displaystyle=h_{p^{\prime}}(\top\ast\beta)\star h_{p}(\alpha)=h_{p^{\prime}}((\top\ast\beta)^{\prime})\star h_{p}(\alpha)

\displaystyle=h_{p}(\top\ast\beta)\star h_{p}(\alpha)=h_{p}(\top\ast\beta\ast\alpha)=\mathds{B}_{(\top\ast\beta\ast\alpha)\otimes\top}(p).

Based on this observation, we refer to (
5.2
) and observe that every left-sided open
\mathfrak{Q}
-presheaf
f
on
\sigma(\mathds{L}(\mathfrak{Q}))
has the form

f=\mathds{B}_{\alpha_{1}\otimes\top}\vee(\mathds{B}_{\alpha_{2}\otimes\top}\star a_{\ell})
. Since
\mathds{B}_{\alpha_{1}\otimes\top}\star a_{\ell}\leq\mathds{B}_{\alpha_{1}\otimes\top}
, we obtain:

f=\mathds{B}_{\alpha_{1}\otimes\top}\vee(\mathds{B}_{(\alpha_{1}\vee\alpha_{2})\otimes\top}\star a_{\ell})=\mathds{B}_{\alpha_{1}\otimes\top}\vee(\mathds{B}_{(\alpha_{1}\vee\alpha_{2})\otimes\top}\star\top\star a_{\ell})=\mathds{B}_{\alpha_{1}\otimes\top}\vee(\mathds{B}_{((\alpha_{1}\vee\alpha_{2})\ast\top)\otimes\top}\star a_{\ell}).

Taking
\alpha:=\alpha_{1}
and
\gamma:=(\alpha_{1}\vee\alpha_{2})\ast\top
the existence is verified.

Now we consider two pairs
(\alpha,\gamma),\,(\overline{\alpha},\overline{\gamma})\in\mathds{L}(\mathfrak{Q})\times\mathds{I}(\mathfrak{Q})

satisfying (
5.3
). Since
\alpha\leq\gamma
and
\overline{\alpha}\leq\overline{\gamma}
, Remark
5.1
implies that for each prime element
p
of
\mathds{L}({\mathfrak{Q}})
the following equivalences hold:

\displaystyle\alpha\not\leq p

\displaystyle\iff(\mathds{B}_{\alpha\otimes\top}\vee(\mathds{B}_{\gamma\otimes\top}\ast a_{\ell}))(p)=(\mathds{B}_{\overline{\alpha}\otimes\top}\vee(\mathds{B}_{\overline{\gamma}\otimes\top}\ast a_{\ell}))(p)=\top\iff\overline{\alpha}\not\leq p\quad\text{and}

\displaystyle\gamma\leq p

\displaystyle\iff(\mathds{B}_{\alpha\otimes\top}\vee(\mathds{B}_{\gamma\otimes\top}\ast a_{\ell}))(p)=(\mathds{B}_{\overline{\alpha}\otimes\top}\vee(\mathds{B}_{\overline{\gamma}\otimes\top}\ast a_{\ell}))(p)=\bot\iff\overline{\gamma}\leq p

hold. Since
\mathds{L}(\mathfrak{Q})
is spatial,
\alpha=\overline{\alpha}
and
\gamma=\overline{\gamma}
follow, and the uniqueness is verified.

(c) Since
\mathds{L}(\mathfrak{Q})
is spatial and a factor, then for all
\alpha\in\mathds{L}(\mathfrak{Q})\setminus\{\bot\}
we conclude that the property
\mathds{I}(\mathfrak{Q})=\{\,\bot,\top\,\}
is equivalent to
\underline{a_{\ell}}\leq\mathds{B}_{\alpha\otimes\top}
. Hence the assertion follows from (b).
∎

Addition
.

Since quantic frames are involutive quantales, the respective assertions of Theorem
5.2
(b) and (c) hold also for right-sided open
\mathfrak{Q}
-presheaves and for
\mathds{R}(\mathfrak{Q})
.

To describe strongly Hausdorff separated, involutive quantized topological subspaces of
\sigma(\mathds{L}(\mathfrak{Q}))
, we need some more terminology.

Let
\mathfrak{Q}
be a quantic frame and
\mathds{ML}(\mathfrak{Q})
be the set of all maximal left-sided elements of
\mathfrak{Q}
. A nonempty subset
C
of
\mathds{ML}(\mathfrak{Q})
is called
reduced
if for each
\alpha\in C
the relation
\mathop{\textstyle\bigwedge}(C\setminus\{\alpha\})\not\leq\alpha
holds, where the infimum is calculated in
\mathds{L}(\mathfrak{Q})
.

Proposition 5.3
.

Let
\mathfrak{Q}
be a quantic frame,
Y=\sigma(\mathds{L}(\mathfrak{Q}))
, and
(Y,\mathcal{T}_{Y})
be the involutive quantized topological space associated with
\mathfrak{Q}

(
cf.
(
5.2
))
. If
C\subseteq\mathds{ML}(\mathfrak{Q})
is reduced, then the relative involutive quantized topological subspace
(C,\mathcal{T}_{C})
is strongly Hausdorff separated.

Proof.

Let us choose
\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}\in C
with
\alpha_{1}\neq\alpha_{2}
. Then we introduce the following elements
\beta_{1},\beta_{2}\in\mathds{L}(\mathfrak{Q})
:

\beta_{1}=\mathop{\textstyle\bigwedge}(C\setminus\{\alpha_{1}\})\quad\text{and}\quad\beta_{2}=\mathop{\textstyle\bigwedge}(C\setminus\{\alpha_{2}\}).

Then
f_{1}=\mathds{B}_{\beta_{1}\otimes\top}\in\mathcal{T}_{C}
is left-sided and
f_{2}=\mathds{B}_{\top\otimes\beta_{2}^{\prime}}\in\mathcal{T}_{C}
is right-sided with
f_{2}(\alpha_{2})\star f_{1}(\alpha_{1})=\top
, and

\displaystyle\mathop{\textstyle\bigvee}\limits_{\alpha\in C}f_{2}(\alpha)\star f_{1}(\alpha)

\displaystyle=\bigl(\mathop{\textstyle\bigvee}\limits_{\alpha\in C\setminus\{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}\}}f_{2}(\alpha)\star f_{1}(\alpha)\bigr)\vee\bigl(f_{2}(\alpha_{1})\star f_{1}(\alpha_{1})\bigr)\vee\bigl(f_{2}(\alpha_{2})\star f_{1}(\alpha_{2})\bigr)

\displaystyle\leq(a_{r}\star a_{\ell})\vee(\top\star a_{\ell})\vee(a_{r}\star\top)=b\vee a_{\ell}\vee a_{r}=c

Hence,
(C,\mathcal{T}_{C})
is strongly Hausdorff separated.
∎

We finish this section with two examples of involutive quantized topological spaces induced by quantic frames.

Example 5.4
.

Recall that
\mathfrak{Q}_{\boldsymbol{2}}
is a quantic frame (cf. Remark
2.9
(b)) with

\mathds{L}(\mathfrak{Q}_{\boldsymbol{2}})=\{\,\bot,a_{\ell},\top\,\}
and
\sigma(\mathds{L}(\mathfrak{Q}_{\boldsymbol{2}}))=\{\,\bot,a_{\ell}\,\}
. Thus
\mathds{L}(\mathfrak{Q}_{\boldsymbol{2}})
is spatial. According to (
2.7
) and (
2.8
), the involutive quantized topology
\mathcal{T}
on
\sigma(\mathds{L}(\mathfrak{Q}_{\boldsymbol{2}}))
associated with
\mathfrak{Q}_{\boldsymbol{2}}
in the sense of (
5.2
) has a base
\mathcal{B}
consisting of
9
elements:

\mathcal{B}=\{\,\mathds{B}_{a_{\ell}\otimes\top},\,\mathds{B}_{\top\otimes a_{r}},\,\mathds{B}_{a_{\ell}\otimes a_{r}},\,a_{r}\star\mathds{B}_{a_{\ell}\otimes\top},\,\mathds{B}_{\top\otimes a_{r}}\star a_{\ell},\,\underline{b},\,\underline{a_{\ell}},\,\underline{a_{r}},\,\underline{\top}\,\}.

Then
\bigl(\sigma(\mathds{L}(\mathfrak{Q}_{\boldsymbol{2}})),\mathcal{T}\bigr)
is an involutive
\mathfrak{Q}_{\boldsymbol{2}}
-sober space (cf. Proposition
4.3
). However, it is easy to check that
f(a_{\ell})\leq f(\bot)
for all
f\in\mathcal{T}
. Hence

\bigl(\sigma(\mathds{L}(\mathfrak{Q}_{\boldsymbol{2}})),\mathcal{T}\bigr)
is not Fréchet separated.

Moreover,
\mathcal{T}
is an involutive quantale, but
not
a quantic frame. Indeed, let us assume that
\mathcal{T}
is a quantic frame. Since
\mathds{L}(\mathfrak{Q}_{\boldsymbol{2}})
is spatial, we conclude from Proposition
5.2
(c) that the subquantale of all left-sided (resp. right-sided) open
\mathfrak{Q}
-presheaves have the following form:

\mathds{L}(\mathcal{T})=\bigl\{\,\underline{\bot},\underline{a_{\ell}},\mathds{B}_{a_{\ell}\otimes\top},\underline{\top}\,\bigr\}\quad\text{and}\quad\mathds{R}(\mathcal{T})=\bigl\{\,\underline{\bot},\underline{a_{r}},\mathds{B}_{\top\otimes a_{r}},\underline{\top}\,\bigr\}.

Hence
\mathds{I}(\mathcal{T})=\{\,\underline{\bot},\overline{\top}\,\}
, and we conclude from Remark
2.9
that
\mathds{L}(\mathcal{T})\otimes\mathds{R}(\mathcal{T})\mathrel{\hbox to14.68pt{\vbox to13.05pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 4.33778pt\lower-5.01602pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.7pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{{}}{}

{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{3.0pt}{-1.68301pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 4.15 -2.33)} \pgfsys@hbox{58}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
